Rule 29.11(b): the motion must be filed 'within fifteen days after the return of the verdict.' 'The court may extend this period by up to ten additional days …

if the defendant applies within the initial fifteen-day window and demonstrates good cause.' Rule 29.11(g): 'An unresolved new trial motion is denied for all purposes if not addressed within ninety days of filing, excluding days when the court lacks authority to act.' Rule 29.11(f): affidavits may be filed and shall be served with the motion; the opposing party has ten days to respond, extendable to thirty days total by court order or stipulation. Rule 29.11(c): no judgment may be entered until the new-trial deadline passes.

Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 29.11 (adopted June 13, 1979, eff. Jan. 1, 1980; amended Aug. 1, 1979; Aug. 19, 1994, eff. July 1, 1995; Dec. 18, 2001, eff. July 1, 2002)

#### Motion for new trial

Post-trialVerified

The central post-trial motion. In a JURY-tried case it is also the document that preserves nearly every claim of error for appeal.

When to use it After a guilty verdict in a jury trial. Optional (but permitted) in a court-tried case.

If you miss it TOTAL WAIVER of nearly every trial error. In a jury case, error not in this motion is reviewable only for plain error under Rule 29.12(b) / 30.20. This is the single most consequential deadline in a Missouri criminal case after conviction.

Notes & source

Pro se note ABSOLUTE OUTER LIMIT 25 DAYS (15 + 10), and Rule 20.01(b) says the court 'may not enlarge the period for filing a motion for new trial or for taking an appeal.' Even the judge cannot save you past 25 days. The 15 days run from the RETURN OF THE VERDICT, not from sentencing. Rule 29.11(d) exempts only three things from the inclusion requirement in jury cases: jurisdiction over the offense, whether the indictment/information states an offense, and sufficiency of the evidence. Everything else - every evidentiary ruling, every instruction, every closing-argument objection - must be written into this motion, with specificity.

Source read [www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199351](https://www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199351)

Rule 29.11(e): the defendant may file such a motion 'within the timeframe in subsection (b)' (fifteen days after the finding …

extendable by up to ten days on application made within the fifteen), 'though not required. A new trial motion is unnecessary to preserve appellate review issues in non-jury trials,' except that the same three exceptions in 29.11(d) apply if one is filed.

Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 29.11(e) (amended Aug. 19, 1994, eff. July 1, 1995; Dec. 18, 2001, eff. July 1, 2002)

#### Motion for new trial in a court-tried (bench) case

Post-trialVerified

The optional new-trial motion where the judge, not a jury, was the fact-finder.

When to use it After a guilty finding in a bench trial - including most misdemeanor trials where a jury was waived.

If you miss it Nothing is automatically waived in a bench trial - but you also gain nothing by silence, and a motion is your only chance to have the trial judge fix the error first.

Notes & source

Pro se note This is a genuine advantage of a bench trial for a pro se defendant: you do not forfeit your appellate issues by failing to file a new-trial motion. Do not rely on it blindly - if you DO file one, make it complete, and objections at trial are still required to preserve evidentiary points.

Source read [www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199351](https://www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199351)

Rule 29.13(a): 'Within thirty days after the entry of the judgment and prior to the filing of the transcript of the record in the appellate court …

the court may of its own initiative or on motion of a defendant arrest or set aside a judgment upon either of the following grounds: (1) that the facts stated in the indictment or information do not constitute an offense; or (2) that the court is without jurisdiction of the offense charged. The court shall specify of record the grounds upon which the order is entered.' Rule 29.13(b): the court may, with the defendant's consent, order a new trial on its own initiative before entry of judgment 'but not later than thirty days after the verdict of the jury is returned.'

Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 29.13 (adopted June 13, 1979, eff. Jan. 1, 1980)

#### Motion in arrest of judgment / to set aside the judgment

Post-trialVerified

A motion attacking the judgment on the ground that the charging document does not state an offense or that the court lacked jurisdiction over the offense. These are the two defects that never waive.

When to use it After conviction, within the 30-day window, where the information fails to charge a crime.

If you miss it You lose this specific trial-court remedy - though the same two grounds survive on appeal under Rule 30.20 and can be noticed at any time under Rule 24.04(b)2.

Notes & source

Pro se note This is the current home of what older sources call the 'motion in arrest of judgment.' Two hard limits: THIRTY DAYS after entry of judgment, AND before the transcript is filed in the appellate court - whichever comes first.

Source read [www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199353](https://www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199353)

No deadline stated in the rule. Rule 29.05 in full …

'The court shall have power to reduce the punishment within the statutory limits prescribed for the offense if it finds that the punishment is excessive.'

Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 29.05 (adopted June 13, 1979, eff. Jan. 1, 1980)

#### Motion to reduce punishment as excessive

SentencingVerified

A short, direct request that the trial court cut the sentence within the statutory range because it is excessive.

When to use it At or immediately after sentencing, or with the post-trial motions.

If you miss it Nothing is waived by the rule's terms, but as a practical matter the trial court loses authority once the judgment becomes final and the appeal is taken.

Notes & source

Pro se note The rule sets no deadline, which is unusual and useful - but do not rely on it after the judgment is final. File it with your Rule 29.11 motion. It only permits reduction WITHIN the statutory range, so identify the range for the class of misdemeanor and where your sentence sits in it.

Source read [www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199345](https://www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199345)

No deadline stated in the statute. RSMo 558.046 …

'The sentencing court may, upon petition, reduce any term of sentence or probation pronounced by the court or a term of conditional release or parole pronounced by the parole board if the court determines that:' (1) the offense did not involve violence or the threat of violence AND involved alcohol or illegal drugs; (2) since the offense, the person has successfully completed a detoxification and rehabilitation program; and (3) the person is not a prior, persistent, dangerous, persistent misdemeanor, or persistent sexual offender.

RSMo 558.046, version effective Aug. 28, 2021 (the version in force on Sept. 9, 2026; a new version takes effect Jan. 1, 2028)

#### Petition to reduce a term of sentence (substance-related offenses)

SentencingVerified

A statutory petition to the sentencing court to reduce a sentence, term of probation, conditional release, or parole for a defendant who has completed a detoxification and rehabilitation program.

When to use it Non-violent offenses that involved alcohol or illegal drugs, where you have since completed treatment.

If you miss it Nothing - there is no deadline - but the relief is discretionary and the eligibility criteria are strict and conjunctive.

Notes & source

Pro se note All three conditions must be met. The 'did not involve violence or the threat of violence' condition will likely exclude a 565.056 assault count and probably a 575.150 resisting count. CAUTION: the Revisor's site currently displays a version effective January 1, 2028; the text above is the version in force today (bid 50131).

Source read [revisor.mo.gov/main/OneSection.aspx?section=558.046](https://revisor.mo.gov/main/OneSection.aspx?section=558.046)

'[A] circuit court only upon its own motion and not that of the state or the offender shall have the power to grant probation to an offender anytime up to one …

hundred twenty days after such offender has been delivered to the department of corrections but not thereafter.' The 120 days run from DELIVERY TO DOC.

RSMo 559.115, version effective Aug. 28, 2022 (the version in force on Sept. 9, 2026; a new version takes effect Jan. 1, 2028)

#### 120-day callback / shock probation

SentencingVerified

The window in which the sentencing court keeps power to pull a defendant back from the Department of Corrections and place him on probation.

When to use it Only where a prison sentence has been executed and the defendant has been delivered to DOC.

If you miss it The court's power evaporates at 120 days - 'but not thereafter.' It is jurisdictional.

Notes & source

Pro se note Read the operative words carefully: the court may act ONLY UPON ITS OWN MOTION, 'and not that of the state or the offender.' You cannot file a motion that compels a ruling. What you can do is file a request/suggestion asking the court to act on its own motion, and get it in front of the judge with time to spare - well before day 120. NOTE: this is largely irrelevant to a misdemeanor case with no DOC sentence. CAUTION: the Revisor's site currently displays a version effective January 1, 2028; the text above is the version in force today (bid 51058).

Source read [revisor.mo.gov/main/OneSection.aspx?section=559.115](https://revisor.mo.gov/main/OneSection.aspx?section=559.115)

Rule 29.18(d): a defendant arrested and confined on a probation-violation warrant must be brought before a judge 'forthwith …

but no later than seven days, excluding weekends and holidays, after the defendant is confined in the county that issued the warrant,' and the court shall consider conditional release under Rule 33.01. Rule 29.18(c): an application for change of judge is governed by Rule 51.05 and 'must be filed within 60 days from issuance of the notice of the intent to revoke' - and no change is allowed from the judge who presided over the underlying criminal case. Rule 29.18(e): 'A court may revoke probation upon compliance with section 559.036, RSMo, but not otherwise.' RSMo 559.036: 'Probation shall not be revoked without giving the probationer notice and an opportunity to be heard on the issues of whether such probationer violated a condition of probation,' and the court must inform the probationer at least FIVE BUSINESS DAYS before the hearing of the right to request appointed counsel. The court retains power to revoke after the probation term expires only 'for any further period which is reasonably necessary for the adjudication of matters arising before its expiration, provided that some affirmative manifestation of an intent to conduct a revocation hearing occurs prior to the expiration of the period.'

Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 29.18 (adopted May 19, 2010, eff. Jan. 1, 2011; amended June 25, 2019, eff. Jan. 1, 2020; Dec. 23, 2024, eff. July 1, 2025); RSMo 559.036 (effective Aug. 28, 2022); Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 24.037 (post-plea revocations governed by Rule 29.18)

#### Probation revocation - demand for notice, hearing, and counsel

SentencingVerified

Not really a motion, but the set of rights you invoke when the state moves to revoke probation, plus the rules governing the hearing.

When to use it When a motion to revoke probation or judicial parole is filed against you.

If you miss it You are revoked without a hearing, without counsel, or after the court has lost authority - and the loss-of-authority point is waivable if you do not raise it.

Notes & source

Pro se note Check the calendar first: if your probation term already expired, the court can only proceed if it made an 'affirmative manifestation of an intent to conduct a revocation hearing' BEFORE expiration. That is a real, frequently successful defense. Also note the change-of-judge window here is 60 days from the notice of intent to revoke, under Rule 51.05 - different rule and different clock from Rule 32.07.

Source read [www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199358](https://www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199358)

RSMo 610.140.6: 'At the time the petition is filed …

it has been at least three years if the offense is a felony, or at least one year if the offense is a misdemeanor, municipal violation, or infraction, from the date the petitioner completed any authorized disposition imposed under section 557.011 for each crime listed in the petition.' Additional criteria: no other misdemeanor or felony convictions during the waiting period (excluding traffic), all obligations satisfied, no pending charges, petitioner not a threat to public safety, and expungement consistent with the public welfare. Lifetime caps under 610.140.13: not more than three misdemeanors or ordinance violations, and not more than two felonies; infractions are not capped. For an arrest with no charge, a petition may be filed after 18 months.

RSMo 610.140 (effective Jan. 1, 2025)

#### Petition for expungement of records

Post-convictionVerified

A civil PETITION (not a motion in the criminal case) filed in a court where you were charged or found guilty, asking that the records be expunged.

When to use it After the waiting period runs and all obligations are satisfied.

If you miss it Nothing is lost by waiting, but the record stays public and reachable by employers and landlords.

Notes & source

Pro se note READ THIS CAREFULLY FOR YOUR CHARGES. Section 455.085 is on the EXCLUSION list at RSMo 610.140.3(6) - a conviction for violating an order of protection is NOT expungeable. Section 610.140.3(5) also excludes 'Any felony offense of assault; misdemeanor or felony offense of domestic assault; or felony offense of kidnapping.' RSMo 575.150 (resisting arrest) and 565.056 (assault in the fourth degree) do NOT appear on the exclusion list - so a misdemeanor conviction on those counts is generally expungeable after one year, but a 455.085 conviction is not. That asymmetry is a real reason to fight the 455.085 count hardest. The waiting period runs from COMPLETION of the disposition (end of probation), not from conviction.

Source read [revisor.mo.gov/main/OneSection.aspx?section=610.140](https://revisor.mo.gov/main/OneSection.aspx?section=610.140)

### Appeal and post-conviction 10

Rule 30.01(a): 'After the rendition of final judgment in a criminal case, every party shall be entitled to any appeal permitted by law.

Appeals may be taken as provided in Rule 81.04 and Rule 81.08.' Rule 81.04(a): 'No such appeal shall be effective unless the notice of appeal shall be filed not later than ten days after the judgment, decree, or order appealed from becomes final.' Rule 30.03 confirms the figure: 'notice of appeal is not filed with the clerk of the trial court within ten days after the judgment becomes final.' Docket fee under Rule 81.04(d) is $70, or file a motion to proceed in forma pauperis (Rule 81.04(e)-(f)).

Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 30.01 (amended through May 19, 2016, eff. Jan. 1, 2017); Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 81.04 (amended through May 19, 2016, eff. Jan. 1, 2017)

#### Notice of appeal

AppellateVerified

The single document that creates the appeal. It is filed in the TRIAL court, not the appellate court.

When to use it After final judgment in a criminal case - which in a criminal case means after sentence is entered.

If you miss it You lose the appeal outright. Rule 20.01(b) says the court 'may not enlarge the period for filing a motion for new trial or for taking an appeal' - the trial court cannot fix this. Your only remedy is the Rule 30.03 special order.

Notes & source

Pro se note TEN DAYS - and note the 2016 amendment: Rule 30.01 no longer states the deadline itself, it now cross-references Rule 81.04. The hard part is figuring out when the judgment 'becomes final.' In a criminal case the judgment is the sentence; a TIMELY motion for new trial delays finality until it is ruled (or until it is deemed denied at 90 days under Rule 29.11(g)). Rule 81.05's 30-day civil finality rule is written for civil matters. When in doubt, file EARLY - a premature notice of appeal is treated as filed immediately after the judgment becomes final (Rule 81.05).

Source read [www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199362](https://www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199362)

'[I]f, within twelve months after the judgment becomes final …

a motion for leave to file such notice is filed in the appropriate appellate court and it thereafter sustains the motion and grants such leave. Such special order may be made by the appellate court, in its discretion, for good cause shown. The order shall specify the time within which the notice of appeal is to be filed in the trial court.'

Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 30.03 (adopted June 13, 1979, eff. Jan. 1, 1980)

#### Motion for leave to file notice of appeal out of time (special order)

AppellateVerified

The rescue provision when you missed the ten days. It is filed in the APPELLATE court, and if granted the appellate court sends you back to file the notice in the trial court.

When to use it As soon as you realize the ten days passed.

If you miss it After twelve months the right to appeal is gone permanently, with no exception in the rule.

Notes & source

Pro se note TWELVE MONTHS is an absolute outer wall. File it in the COURT OF APPEALS (Eastern District for Marion County - verify the district for your county), not the circuit court, and address 'good cause' explicitly. This same rule covers appeals from orders in post-conviction proceedings involving a prior felony conviction.

Source read [www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199364](https://www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199364)

No numeric deadline. Rule 30.16: 'If a convicted defendant is entitled to a conditional release pending an appeal …

the conditions shall be determined by the trial court pursuant to Rule 33. A condition shall be that the defendant will render himself or herself in execution and will obey every order and judgment of the appellate court ...' The application must be signed, acknowledged, filed in the circuit clerk's office, and specify a post office address for all notices; proof of mailing to that address 'shall constitute sufficient notice to defendant in all cases where notice is required under these rules.'

Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 30.16 (adopted June 13, 1979, eff. Jan. 1, 1980; amended Jan. 25, 1994, eff. Sept. 1, 1994); conditions set under Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 33

#### Application for release pending appeal

AppellateVerified

A request to remain at liberty while the appeal is pending. Conditions are set by the TRIAL court under Rule 33, not by the appellate court.

When to use it After conviction and sentence, once the notice of appeal is filed.

If you miss it You serve the sentence while the appeal runs, and if you win the appeal the time is already gone.

Notes & source

Pro se note Two practical points. (1) You apply to the TRIAL court even though the appeal is in the appellate court. (2) The address you put in the application becomes the legal address for ALL notices in the case - if you move and do not update it (see Rule 33.16, Notice of Change of Address), you can be defaulted on notices you never received.

Source read [www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199377](https://www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199377)

Rule 24.035(b): if NO appeal is taken, the motion must be filed 'within 180 days of the date the sentence is entered.' If an appeal IS taken …

'within 90 days after the date the mandate of the appellate court issues affirming such judgment or sentence.' On remand with no subsequent appeal: 'within 180 days of the date the court's resulting judgment or sentence is entered.' On remand with a subsequent appeal: 'within 90 days after the date the mandate of the appellate court issues affirming the judgment or sentence entered after remand.' A motion mailed with proper postage and a legible postmark is timely if deposited by the deadline. 'Failure to file a motion within the time provided by this Rule 24.035 shall constitute a complete waiver of any right to proceed under this Rule 24.035.' Rule 24.035(e): counsel must be appointed for an indigent movant 'within 30 days after an indigent movant files a pro se motion.' Rule 24.035(l): 'The circuit court shall not entertain successive motions.'

Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 24.035 (adopted Feb. 11, 1987, eff. Jan. 1, 1988; amended through Dec. 20, 2022, eff. July 1, 2023)

#### Rule 24.035 motion to vacate, set aside, or correct judgment or sentence (after a guilty plea)

Post-convictionVerified

Missouri's exclusive post-conviction remedy for someone convicted of a FELONY on a guilty plea. Filed on Criminal Procedure Form No. 40 in the sentencing court.

When to use it After a felony guilty plea, to raise constitutional violations, lack of jurisdiction, ineffective assistance of plea counsel, or a sentence in excess of the maximum.

If you miss it COMPLETE WAIVER, by the rule's own words. Missouri courts enforce these deadlines strictly; there is no 'excusable neglect' escape and Rule 20.01(b) does not help.

Notes & source

Pro se note TWO CORRECTIONS TO WIDELY REPEATED MISINFORMATION. (1) The trigger is the DATE THE SENTENCE IS ENTERED (or the date the mandate issues), NOT the date of delivery to the Department of Corrections - that was the pre-2018 rule. (2) Rule 24.035(a) covers 'A person convicted of a FELONY on a plea of guilty.' A MISDEMEANOR conviction has NO Rule 24.035 remedy at all. For a misdemeanant, the alternative collateral remedy is a petition for a writ of habeas corpus under Rule 91 (which requires restraint of liberty), or a direct appeal. Rule 24.035(d) requires you to list EVERY known claim and to declare that you understand you waive anything you leave out - so the pro se motion must be complete on day one.

Source read [www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199272](https://www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199272)

Rule 24.035(g): if NO appeal was taken, the amended motion must be filed 'within 120 days of the earlier of the date both a complete transcript consisting of …

the guilty plea and sentencing hearing has been filed in the trial court and' either (1) counsel is appointed, or (2) an entry of appearance is filed by any counsel not appointed but who chooses to appear. If an appeal WAS taken: 'within 120 days of the earlier of the date both the mandate of the appellate court is issued and' counsel is appointed or enters an appearance. 'The court may not extend the time for filing the amended motion or statement in lieu of an amended motion.' An amended motion may not incorporate prior filings by reference or attachment - all claims must appear in its body. The prosecutor responds 'within 30 days after the date an amended motion or statement in lieu of amended motion is required to be filed.'

Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 24.035(g) (amended Dec. 20, 2022, eff. July 1, 2023)

#### Rule 24.035 amended motion (after appointment of counsel)

Post-convictionNo extensionVerified

The counseled amended motion that supersedes the pro se motion. It must be self-contained.

When to use it After counsel is appointed or enters an appearance on a timely pro se motion.

If you miss it The court must inquire on the record whether the movant was abandoned by counsel; if there was no abandonment, the untimely amended claims are lost and only the pro se motion's claims survive.

Notes & source

Pro se note IMPORTANT CORRECTION: the current rule gives 120 DAYS with NO EXTENSION. The older '60 days plus one 30-day extension' formulation is OUT OF DATE - it was superseded by the amendments effective July 1, 2023. Verify this against the rule text yourself before relying on any secondary source that still says 60+30.

Source read [www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199272](https://www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199272)

Rule 29.15(b): 'The motion shall be filed no earlier than the date the sentence is entered if no appeal is taken ...

or the date the mandate of the appellate court issues affirming the judgment or sentence.' If no appeal: 'within 180 days of the date the sentence is entered.' If an appeal was taken and affirmed: 'within 90 days after the date the mandate of the appellate court issues.' On remand without further appeal: 'within 180 days of the date the court's resulting judgment or sentence is entered.' On remand with appeal: 'within 90 days after the date the mandate of the appellate court issues affirming the judgment.' 'Failure to file a motion within the time provided by this Rule 29.15 shall constitute a complete waiver of any right to proceed under this Rule 29.15.' Rule 29.15(e): counsel appointed for an indigent movant within 30 days of filing. Rule 29.15(l): 'The circuit court shall not entertain successive motions.'

Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 29.15 (adopted Feb. 11, 1987, eff. Jan. 1, 1988; amended through Dec. 20, 2022, eff. July 1, 2023)

#### Rule 29.15 motion to vacate, set aside, or correct judgment or sentence (after trial)

Post-convictionVerified

The mirror image of Rule 24.035 for someone convicted of a FELONY after trial. Also filed on Form No. 40 in the sentencing court.

When to use it After a felony trial conviction, to raise ineffective assistance of trial or appellate counsel and constitutional claims that could not be raised on direct appeal.

If you miss it COMPLETE WAIVER. Strictly enforced.

Notes & source

Pro se note Same two corrections as Rule 24.035: the trigger is entry of sentence or issuance of the mandate (NOT delivery to DOC, and NOT the filing of the transcript in the appeal), and Rule 29.15(a) covers 'A person convicted of a FELONY after trial.' No felony, no Rule 29.15. If you were convicted of a misdemeanor and sentenced to no prison time, there is no 24.035/29.15 remedy - your routes are the direct appeal and, if you are restrained of liberty, a Rule 91 habeas petition. Note the published Rule 29.15(m) 'Schedule' subsection oddly cross-references Rule 24.035; that appears to be a drafting artifact in the official text.

Source read [www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199355](https://www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199355)

Rule 29.15(g): if no appeal was taken, 'within 120 days of the earlier of the date both a complete transcript of the trial and sentencing hearing has been …

filed in the trial court and: (1) Counsel is appointed, or (2) An entry of appearance is filed by any counsel.' If an appeal was taken, 'within 120 days of the earlier of the date both the mandate of the appellate court is issued and: (1) Counsel is appointed, or (2) An entry of appearance is filed.' 'The court may not extend the time for filing the amended motion or statement in lieu of an amended motion.' The prosecutor responds within 30 days of the amended-motion deadline.

Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 29.15(g) (amended Dec. 20, 2022, eff. July 1, 2023)

#### Rule 29.15 amended motion (after appointment of counsel)

Post-convictionNo extensionVerified

The counseled amended motion in a post-trial post-conviction case.

When to use it After counsel is appointed or enters an appearance on a timely pro se Rule 29.15 motion.

If you miss it If the amended motion is late, the court must inquire on the record into abandonment by counsel; absent abandonment, only the pro se claims are adjudicated.

Notes & source

Pro se note 120 DAYS, NO EXTENSION - the '60 days plus a 30-day extension' rule is out of date. Note the deadline runs from the EARLIER of two composite events, each of which requires BOTH the transcript/mandate AND the counsel event - read the 'both ... and' construction carefully.

Source read [www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199355](https://www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199355)

No deadline stated in the rule. Rule 91.01(b) …

'Any person restrained of liberty within this state may petition for a writ of habeas corpus to inquire into the cause of such restraint.' Rule 91.01(a): proceedings in a circuit court are governed by Rule 91; in the Supreme Court or court of appeals, by Rules 84.22 to 84.26 together with Rule 91. Rule 91.01(c): 'A habeas corpus proceeding shall be a civil action in which the person seeking relief is petitioner and the person against whom such relief is sought is respondent.'

Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 91.01 (adopted June 24, 1982; amended Sept. 28, 1993, eff. Jan. 1, 1994; Oct. 22, 1996, eff. July 1, 1997)

#### Petition for writ of habeas corpus (Rule 91)

Post-convictionVerified

The general habeas remedy. For a misdemeanant with no Rule 24.035/29.15 route, this is the principal collateral remedy - but it requires that you be restrained of liberty.

When to use it When you are restrained of liberty (in custody, and in some circumstances on probation or parole) and the conviction or restraint is unlawful.

If you miss it No deadline is stated, but habeas is an extraordinary remedy and Missouri courts require you to have exhausted the ordinary routes (direct appeal, and Rule 24.035/29.15 where available) first.

Notes & source

Pro se note The threshold requirement is RESTRAINT OF LIBERTY. A misdemeanor conviction with a fine and no custody generally will not support Rule 91 relief - which means for such a conviction the direct appeal really is the only meaningful route, and the 10-day notice of appeal is everything. Note the filing sequence: normally file in the circuit court of the county of restraint first; original writs in the appellate courts go under Rules 84.22-84.26 (and Rule 30.31).

Source read [www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199893](https://www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199893)

No deadline. Rule 30.20: 'Allegations of error that are not briefed or are not properly briefed on appeal shall not be considered by the appellate court except …

errors respecting the sufficiency of the information or indictment, verdict, judgment, or sentence. Whether briefed or not, plain errors affecting substantial rights may be considered in the discretion of the court when the court finds that manifest injustice or miscarriage of justice has resulted therefrom.' The trial-court analogue is Rule 29.12(b), in identical terms.

Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 30.20 (adopted June 13, 1979, eff. Jan. 1, 1980; amended June 23, 1987, eff. Jan. 1, 1988); Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 29.12

#### Plain error review on appeal

AppellateVerified

Not a motion but the standard that governs anything you failed to preserve. Knowing it tells you exactly what preservation is worth.

When to use it On appeal, for issues not objected to at trial or not raised in the motion for new trial.

If you miss it Plain error is discretionary and rarely granted - it is a safety net with very large holes.

Notes & source

Pro se note Read the first sentence closely: sufficiency of the information/indictment, the verdict, the judgment, and the sentence are considered EVEN IF NOT BRIEFED. Everything else needs proper briefing plus preservation, or discretionary plain-error review requiring 'manifest injustice.' This is why the Rule 29.11 motion for new trial matters so much.

Source read [www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199381](https://www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199381)

UNVERIFIED - I was unable to load Rule 30.26 ('Post-Disposition Motions') this session;

the page returned repeated 403 errors. Rule 30.26 is the correct rule number and title (confirmed from the official Rule 30 index). Do NOT rely on any remembered number of days. Verify the deadline directly in Rule 30.26 and in Rules 83.02/84.17 before filing.

Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 30.26 (Post-Disposition Motions) - text not verified this session; see also Rules 83.02, 84.17

#### Motion for rehearing / application for transfer after the appellate decision

AppellateUnverified

Post-disposition motions in the court of appeals - rehearing, or transfer to the Missouri Supreme Court.

When to use it After the court of appeals issues its opinion, and before the mandate issues.

If you miss it The mandate issues and the appeal is over; and a timely application for transfer is a prerequisite to Supreme Court review.

Notes & source

Pro se note This deadline is short (a matter of days after the opinion) and it also controls when the mandate issues - which in turn starts the 90-day clock for a Rule 24.035/29.15 motion. Confirm the exact number from the rule text before you rely on it.

Source read [www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199359](https://www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199359)

### Order-of-protection crossover (civil, ch. 455) 8

RSMo 455.035.1: 'Upon the filing of a verified petition ...

and for good cause shown in the petition, the court may immediately issue an ex parte order of protection. An immediate and present danger of domestic violence to the petitioner or the child on whose behalf the petition is filed shall constitute good cause ... An ex parte order of protection entered by the court shall take effect when entered and shall remain in effect until there is valid service of process and a hearing is held on the motion.' 455.035.2: 'Failure to serve an ex parte order of protection on the respondent shall not affect the validity or enforceability of such order.'

RSMo 455.035 (effective Aug. 28, 2013); terms available under RSMo 455.045 (effective Aug. 28, 2021)

#### Ex parte order of protection - issuance and duration

Civil crossoverVerified

The immediate, without-notice order issued on a verified petition showing good cause. It lasts only until service and a hearing.

When to use it Understanding what was entered against you at the outset, and how long it can lawfully last.

If you miss it Violating an ex parte order is itself a class A misdemeanor under RSMo 455.085.7, escalating to a class E felony on a second violation within five years.

Notes & source

Pro se note Note the trap in 455.035.2: failure to SERVE the ex parte order does not make it invalid. But service and knowledge are still central to a criminal prosecution for violating it - RSMo 455.085 requires the respondent to have knowledge. Make the state prove notice.

Source read [revisor.mo.gov/main/OneSection.aspx?section=455.035](https://revisor.mo.gov/main/OneSection.aspx?section=455.035)

RSMo 455.040.1: 'Not later than fifteen days after the filing of a petition that meets the requirements of section 455.020 …

a hearing shall be held unless the court deems, for good cause shown, that a continuance should be granted.'

RSMo 455.040 (effective Aug. 28, 2021, A.L. 2021 S.B. 53 & 60 merged with S.B. 71)

#### Full order of protection hearing - the 15-day rule and continuances

Civil crossoverVerified

The statutory requirement that a hearing on the petition occur within fifteen days, and the good-cause continuance exception that swallows a great deal of it in practice.

When to use it Right after the ex parte order is entered - this is your first and often best chance to defeat the order before it becomes the predicate for a criminal charge.

If you miss it The ex parte order remains in effect during any continuance - so every continuance extends the period during which you can be charged under RSMo 455.085.7 for a violation.

Notes & source

Pro se note This is the highest-leverage hearing in the whole matter, because a full order under RSMo 455.085.8 is what a criminal violation charge is built on. Appear. Bring witnesses. If you need a continuance to prepare, understand the cost: the ex parte order stays live the entire time.

Source read [revisor.mo.gov/main/OneSection.aspx?section=455.040](https://revisor.mo.gov/main/OneSection.aspx?section=455.040)

RSMo 455.040: a full order of protection is 'valid for at least one hundred eighty days and not more than one year.' Where the court makes 'specific written …

findings that the respondent poses a serious danger to the physical or mental health of the petitioner or of a minor household member,' the order may be 'valid for at least two years and not more than ten years.' The petitioner may request renewal after a hearing, subject to the same durational limits, except that in serious-danger cases a renewed order may extend 'up to the life of the respondent.' The statute also authorizes automatic renewal 'unless the respondent requests a hearing by thirty days prior to the expiration.'

RSMo 455.040 (effective Aug. 28, 2021, A.L. 2021 S.B. 53 & 60 merged with S.B. 71)

#### Duration of a full order of protection, and renewal

Civil crossoverVerified

How long a full order lasts, when it can run two to ten years, and how it gets renewed.

When to use it Assessing exposure - the length of the order is the length of your criminal risk under RSMo 455.085.

If you miss it An automatic renewal takes effect and you never get a hearing.

Notes & source

Pro se note CALENDAR THE EXPIRATION DATE AND SUBTRACT 31 DAYS. The automatic-renewal provision requires the RESPONDENT to request a hearing at least thirty days before expiration - miss that and the order renews without any hearing at all. Also note the 2021 amendments created the 2-to-10-year order, which requires SPECIFIC WRITTEN FINDINGS of serious danger; if the court entered a long order without those written findings, that is an appealable defect.

Source read [revisor.mo.gov/main/OneSection.aspx?section=455.040](https://revisor.mo.gov/main/OneSection.aspx?section=455.040)

No deadline. RSMo 455.060.1: 'After notice and hearing …

the court may modify an order of protection at any time, upon subsequent motion filed by the guardian ad litem, the court-appointed special advocate or by either party together with an affidavit showing a change in circumstances sufficient to warrant the modification.'

RSMo 455.060.1 (effective Aug. 28, 2013)

#### Motion to modify a full order of protection

Civil crossoverVerified

A motion, supported by affidavit, asking the court to change the terms of an existing order based on changed circumstances.

When to use it When the order's terms have become unworkable or the circumstances underlying it have changed.

If you miss it You stay bound by terms you could have narrowed - and every one of those terms is a potential RSMo 455.085 criminal charge.

Notes & source

Pro se note The AFFIDAVIT showing changed circumstances is mandatory, not optional - a bare motion will be denied. Narrowing an overbroad order (for example, a no-contact term that criminalizes an unavoidable encounter at a shared workplace or a child exchange) is one of the most practical things you can do to reduce criminal exposure.

Source read [revisor.mo.gov/main/OneSection.aspx?section=455.060](https://revisor.mo.gov/main/OneSection.aspx?section=455.060)

No deadline. RSMo 455.060.5: 'Any order of protection or order for child support …

custody, temporary custody, visitation or maintenance entered under sections 455.010 to 455.085 shall terminate upon the order of the court granting a motion to terminate the order of protection by the petitioner. Prior to terminating any order of protection, the court may inquire of the petitioner or others in order to determine whether the dismissal is voluntary.' RSMo 455.060.4: all provisions terminate on entry of a decree of dissolution or legal separation, except specified counseling and stay-away provisions.

RSMo 455.060.4-.5 (effective Aug. 28, 2013)

#### Motion to terminate an order of protection

Civil crossoverVerified

Termination of the order. Note the statute puts this in the PETITIONER's hands.

When to use it When the protected party wants the order lifted.

If you miss it The order stays in force for its full term.

Notes & source

Pro se note Read 455.060.5 carefully: the termination motion is the PETITIONER's to file, not yours. A respondent's route is modification under 455.060.1 or appeal under 455.060.1, not termination. And be extremely careful about contacting the petitioner to ask for a termination - depending on the order's terms, that contact can itself be the RSMo 455.085 violation.

Source read [revisor.mo.gov/main/OneSection.aspx?section=455.060](https://revisor.mo.gov/main/OneSection.aspx?section=455.060)

RSMo 455.060.1: 'All full orders of protection shall be final orders and appealable and shall be for a fixed period of time as provided in section 455.040.' …

The appeal is a civil appeal: Rule 81.04(a), 'the notice of appeal shall be filed not later than ten days after the judgment, decree, or order appealed from becomes final.' Rule 81.05(a): 'A judgment becomes final in a civil matter at the expiration of 30 days after its entry if no timely authorized after-trial motion is filed'; if such a motion is filed, finality occurs at the earlier of 90 days from the last timely motion or the date the last motion is ruled, provided that is at least 30 days after entry. Rule 78.04: 'Any motion for new trial and any motion to amend the judgment or opinion shall be filed not later than thirty days after the entry of judgment.'

RSMo 455.060.1; Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 81.04 (amended May 19, 2016, eff. Jan. 1, 2017); Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 81.05 (amended Oct. 1, 2024, eff. July 1, 2025); Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 78.04

#### Appeal from a full order of protection

Civil crossoverVerified

A full order of protection is a final, appealable civil judgment. This is the direct route to attacking the order that underlies the criminal charge.

When to use it After the full order is entered.

If you miss it The order stands unreviewed, and it becomes the unchallengeable predicate for the RSMo 455.085 criminal count.

Notes & source

Pro se note The civil timing is DIFFERENT from the criminal timing and it is easy to confuse them. In a civil case the judgment does not become final for 30 days, and then you get 10 more days to file the notice of appeal - so roughly 40 days from entry if you file no after-trial motion. An EX PARTE order is not a final appealable judgment; only the FULL order is. If you also want the trial court to reconsider, a Rule 78.04 motion has its own 30-day deadline and it changes when the judgment becomes final.

Source read [revisor.mo.gov/main/OneSection.aspx?section=455.060](https://revisor.mo.gov/main/OneSection.aspx?section=455.060)

No deadline - this is the charging statute.

RSMo 455.085.7: 'A violation of the terms and conditions ... of an ex parte order of protection ... shall be a class A misdemeanor unless the respondent has previously pleaded guilty to or has been found guilty ... of violating an ex parte order of protection or a full order of protection within five years of the date of the subsequent violation, in which case the subsequent violation shall be a class E felony.' RSMo 455.085.8 applies parallel language to FULL orders: first violation a class A misdemeanor, a subsequent violation within five years a class E felony.

RSMo 455.085 (effective Aug. 28, 2022, A.L. 2022 S.B. 775 et al.)

#### Understanding the criminal exposure created by the order (RSMo 455.085)

Civil crossoverVerified

The statute that turns a civil order into a criminal charge. This is the link between the protection case and the misdemeanor prosecution.

When to use it Understanding what the state must prove on the 455.085 count, and why this count matters more than the others.

If you miss it A conviction here has a consequence the other counts do not: RSMo 610.140.3(6) puts section 455.085 on the list of offenses that are NOT eligible for expungement.

Notes & source

Pro se note This is the count to fight hardest, for two reasons. (1) The five-year escalator: a first conviction turns any future violation into a class E FELONY. (2) Expungement: 455.085 is expressly excluded from expungement under RSMo 610.140.3(6), while resisting arrest (575.150) and fourth-degree assault (565.056) are not on the exclusion list and are generally expungeable one year after completing the disposition. A plea that resolves the 455.085 count with a conviction is permanent in a way the others are not.

Source read [revisor.mo.gov/main/OneSection.aspx?section=455.085](https://revisor.mo.gov/main/OneSection.aspx?section=455.085)

No deadline - these are charging statutes.

RSMo 575.150 (resisting or interfering with arrest): requires that the person KNOW an officer is making an arrest, stop or detention and then resist 'by using or threatening the use of violence or physical force or by fleeing,' or interfere with another's arrest. 'It is no defense to a prosecution pursuant to subsection 1 of this section that the law enforcement officer was acting unlawfully in making the arrest.' Grading: class E felony where the underlying arrest is for a felony, a felony failure-to-appear warrant, or a felony probation-violation warrant, or where fleeing creates a substantial risk of serious injury or death; otherwise a class A misdemeanor. RSMo 565.056 (assault in the fourth degree), version effective Jan. 1, 2017: a person commits the offense if, among other things, he 'attempts to cause or recklessly causes physical injury, physical pain, or illness to another person'; 'purposely places another person in apprehension of immediate physical injury'; or 'knowingly causes physical contact with another person knowing the other person will regard the contact as offensive or provocative.' Grading: 'Assault in the fourth degree is a class A misdemeanor,' except that violations of subdivisions (3) or (6) are class C misdemeanors unless the victim is a 'special victim' as defined in section 565.002, in which case class A.

RSMo 575.150 (effective Aug. 28, 2025, A.L. 2025 H.B. 495); RSMo 565.056 version effective Jan. 1, 2017 (the version in force on Sept. 9, 2026)

#### Reading the underlying charges (575.150 and 565.056)

Civil crossoverVerified

The elements and grading of the other two counts, verified against current text.

When to use it Framing motions to dismiss, sufficiency arguments, and plea evaluation.

If you miss it Not applicable - these define the charges rather than impose deadlines.

Notes & source

Pro se note Two useful points. (1) On 575.150, note what the statute does NOT say: the 'no defense that the officer was acting unlawfully' clause is limited by its terms to subsection 1, and the state must still prove you KNEW an arrest was being made. (2) On 565.056, the grading matters a great deal - a charge under subdivision (3) or (6) with a non-special victim is only a CLASS C misdemeanor. Check which subdivision the information actually pleads; if it does not identify one, that is a Rule 23.04 bill of particulars issue. IMPORTANT: revisor.mo.gov currently displays a version of 565.056 effective July 1, 2027; the text above is the version in force today (bid 29283).

Source read [revisor.mo.gov/main/OneSection.aspx?section=565.056](https://revisor.mo.gov/main/OneSection.aspx?section=565.056)

### Time computation 4

Rule 20.01(a): 'In computing any period of time prescribed or allowed by these Rules …

by order of court, or by any applicable statute, the day of the act, event, or default after which the designated period of time begins to run is not to be included. The last day of the period so computed is to be included, unless it is a Saturday, Sunday, or a legal holiday, in which event the period runs until the end of the next day which is neither a Saturday, Sunday nor a legal holiday. When the period of time prescribed or allowed is less than seven days, intermediate Saturdays, Sundays, and legal holidays shall be excluded in the computation.'

Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 20.01(a) (adopted June 13, 1979, eff. Jan. 1, 1980)

#### Computing a deadline in a criminal case

Time computationNo extensionVerified

The counting rule for every criminal deadline in the Missouri rules, court orders, and applicable statutes.

When to use it Every time you calendar anything.

If you miss it You miscount and file a day late on a deadline that cannot be extended.

Notes & source

Pro se note Three separate mechanics: (1) do not count day zero; (2) if the last day is a weekend or legal holiday, roll forward; (3) ONLY for periods shorter than seven days do you skip intervening weekends and holidays. A 10-day period (change of judge, notice of appeal) counts calendar days straight through, with only the roll-forward at the end. A 5-day period skips weekends throughout.

Source read [www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199229](https://www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199229)

Rule 20.01(b): the court for cause shown may at any time in its discretion '(1) …

with or without motion or notice order the period enlarged if request therefor is made before the expiration of the period originally prescribed or as extended by a previous order or (2) upon notice and motion made after the expiration of the specified period permit the act to be done where the failure to act was the result of excusable neglect; BUT THE COURT MAY NOT ENLARGE THE PERIOD FOR FILING A MOTION FOR NEW TRIAL OR FOR TAKING AN APPEAL AS PROVIDED BY THESE RULES.'

Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 20.01(b) (adopted June 13, 1979, eff. Jan. 1, 1980)

#### Motion to enlarge time - and the deadlines that cannot be enlarged

Time computationVerified

The general extension rule, and its two hard exceptions.

When to use it When you need more time - and to check first whether the deadline is one the court has no power to extend.

If you miss it For most deadlines, 'excusable neglect' can save you even after expiration. For the motion for new trial and the notice of appeal, NOTHING can.

Notes & source

Pro se note Memorize the two exceptions. The motion for new trial is capped at 25 days (15 + 10 under Rule 29.11(b)), and the court cannot go past it. The notice of appeal is 10 days, and the trial court cannot extend it - only the APPELLATE court can, and only via a Rule 30.03 special order within 12 months. Also note the Rule 24.035(g)/29.15(g) amended-motion deadlines carry their own no-extension language.

Source read [www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199229](https://www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199229)

Rule 20.01(d): 'A written motion, other than one which may be heard ex parte …

and notice of the hearing thereof shall be served not later than five days before the time specified for the hearing, unless a different period is fixed by law or court rule or by order of the court. Such an order may for cause shown be made on ex parte application. When a motion is supported by an affidavit, the affidavit shall be served with the motion; and, except as otherwise provided by law or rule in connection with a motion for a new trial, opposing affidavits may be served not later than one day before the hearing, unless the court permits them to be served at some other time.'

Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 20.01(d) (adopted June 13, 1979, eff. Jan. 1, 1980)

#### Notice and service requirements for a written motion

Time computationVerified

How far in advance a written motion and its hearing notice must be served, and the rules for supporting affidavits.

When to use it Every written motion you file that is not heard ex parte.

If you miss it The court can refuse to hear the motion, or continue it, costing you the setting.

Notes & source

Pro se note FIVE DAYS is less than seven, so under Rule 20.01(a) you EXCLUDE intervening Saturdays, Sundays, and legal holidays when counting it - which in practice means about a full calendar week. Any supporting affidavit must go out WITH the motion, not later.

Source read [www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199229](https://www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199229)

Rule 20.01(e): 'When a party has the right or is required to do some act or take some proceedings within a prescribed period after the service of a notice or …

other paper upon him and the notice or paper is served upon him by mail, three days shall be added to the prescribed period.'

Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 20.01(e) (adopted June 13, 1979, eff. Jan. 1, 1980)

#### Three extra days for service by mail

Time computationVerified

The mailbox add-on for deadlines that run from service of a paper on you.

When to use it Whenever your time to act runs from service of a notice or paper that was mailed.

If you miss it You shorten your own deadline by three days unnecessarily.

Notes & source

Pro se note This only applies to periods that run FROM SERVICE of a paper. It does NOT extend deadlines that run from an event - the verdict, the plea, the sentence, or entry of judgment. So it does not add three days to the Rule 29.11 new-trial motion or to the notice of appeal.

Source read [www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199229](https://www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199229)

## Federal civil 77

### Pleading 19

"within 21 days after being served with the summons and complaint" (Rule 12(a)(1)(A)(i)).

Fed. R. Civ. P. 12(a)(1)(A)(i)

#### Answer to a complaint (served personally)

ProceduralVerified

The defendant's responsive pleading admitting or denying each allegation and stating affirmative defenses. Not a motion, but the deadline every Rule 12 motion is measured against.

When to use it After being served with a summons and complaint, if you are not first filing a Rule 12 motion.

If you miss it Plaintiff may seek entry of default under Rule 55(a) and then a default judgment under Rule 55(b).

Notes & source

Response / reply Plaintiff's reply to a counterclaim is due within 21 days after service of the answer (Rule 12(a)(1)(B)).

Pro se note Filing a Rule 12(b) motion instead of an answer suspends the answer deadline; you then get 14 days after the court denies it (Rule 12(a)(4)).

Source read [www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_12](https://www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_12)

"within 60 days after the request for a waiver was sent" …

or "within 90 days after it was sent to the defendant outside any judicial district" of the United States (Rule 12(a)(1)(A)(ii)).

Fed. R. Civ. P. 12(a)(1)(A)(ii); Fed. R. Civ. P. 4(d)

#### Answer after waiver of service

ProceduralVerified

A defendant who signs and returns a Rule 4(d) waiver of service gets a much longer period to answer, as the incentive for waiving.

When to use it When the defendant returned a waiver of service rather than being formally served.

If you miss it Same as any missed answer: exposure to default under Rule 55.

Notes & source

Pro se note The 60 days runs from the date the waiver REQUEST WAS SENT, not from the date the defendant signed or returned it.

Source read [www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_12](https://www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_12)

"60 days after service on the United States attorney." For an officer or employee sued individually for an act connected with federal duties …

60 days after service on the officer or employee OR on the United States attorney, "whichever is later" (Rule 12(a)(2)–(3)).

Fed. R. Civ. P. 12(a)(2), 12(a)(3)

#### Answer by the United States, its agencies, officers, or employees

ProceduralVerified

An extended answer period for federal-government defendants.

When to use it When a defendant is the United States, a federal agency, or a federal officer/employee sued in an official or individual capacity for acts connected with federal duties.

If you miss it Default is available in theory, but Rule 55(d) bars a default judgment against the United States unless the claimant establishes the claim by satisfactory evidence.

Notes & source

Source read [www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_12](https://www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_12)

"within 14 days after notice of the court's action" (Rule 12(a)(4)(A)).

If the court grants a Rule 12(e) motion for a more definite statement, the responsive pleading is due within 14 days after the more definite statement is served (Rule 12(a)(4)(B)).

Fed. R. Civ. P. 12(a)(4)

#### Responsive pleading after the court denies a Rule 12 motion

ProceduralVerified

The restarted answer clock once a pre-answer motion is resolved.

When to use it Immediately after the court denies your Rule 12 motion or postpones its disposition until trial.

If you miss it Default exposure under Rule 55 even though you actively litigated the motion.

Notes & source

Pro se note This 14-day clock is easy to blow because it feels like the case is still 'pending' after a denial. Calendar it the day the order is docketed.

Source read [www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_12](https://www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_12)

No deadline. Rule 12(h)(3): "If the court determines at any time that it lacks subject-matter jurisdiction …

the court must dismiss the action." A pre-answer motion must still be served before the responsive pleading (Rule 12(b)).

Fed. R. Civ. P. 12(b)(1), 12(h)(3)

#### Motion to dismiss for lack of subject-matter jurisdiction (Rule 12(b)(1))

DispositiveVerified

Argues the federal court has no constitutional or statutory power to hear the case at all — e.g., no federal question, no diversity, no standing, or the claim is moot or unripe.

When to use it Whenever the court's power over the subject of the suit is doubtful. In an FMLA/ADA case, federal-question jurisdiction under 28 U.S.C. § 1331 normally supplies it.

If you miss it Nothing is waived — this defense can never be waived or forfeited, and the court must raise it on its own.

Notes & source

Response / reply Set by local rule — E.D. Mo. L.R. 4.01(B): 14 days to oppose; 4.01(C): 10 days to reply. W.D. Mo. L.R. 7.0: 14 days to oppose; 14 days to reply.

Pro se note This is the one defense that survives everything. It also means a judgment you win can be attacked later if jurisdiction was actually absent.

Source read [www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_12](https://www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_12)

Must be raised in the FIRST Rule 12 motion, or — if no Rule 12 motion is made — in the answer or in an amendment allowed as of course under Rule 15(a)(1) (Rule 12(h)(1)).

Fed. R. Civ. P. 12(b)(2), 12(g)(2), 12(h)(1)

#### Motion to dismiss for lack of personal jurisdiction (Rule 12(b)(2))

DispositiveVerified

Argues the court has no power over this particular defendant because of insufficient contacts with the forum state.

When to use it When an out-of-state defendant has no meaningful connection to Missouri.

If you miss it WAIVED. Rule 12(h)(1): a party waives 12(b)(2)–(5) by omitting it from a Rule 12 motion it did file, or by failing to raise it by motion or in the responsive pleading.

Notes & source

Response / reply Set by local rule — E.D. Mo. L.R. 4.01(B)/(C): 14 days / 10 days. W.D. Mo. L.R. 7.0: 14 days / 14 days.

Pro se note If you are the plaintiff, watch for the defendant omitting this from a first motion — that waives it permanently.

Source read [www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_12](https://www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_12)

Must be raised in the first Rule 12 motion, or in the answer / Rule 15(a)(1) amendment (Rule 12(h)(1)).

Fed. R. Civ. P. 12(b)(3), 12(g)(2), 12(h)(1)

#### Motion to dismiss for improper venue (Rule 12(b)(3))

DispositiveVerified

Argues the case was filed in the wrong federal district. Often paired with a request to transfer under 28 U.S.C. § 1406(a) rather than outright dismissal.

When to use it When neither the defendant's residence nor the events giving rise to the claim connect to this district.

If you miss it WAIVED under Rule 12(h)(1).

Notes & source

Response / reply Set by local rule — E.D. Mo. L.R. 4.01(B)/(C); W.D. Mo. L.R. 7.0.

Pro se note Missouri has two federal districts (Eastern and Western). Employment suits are normally venued where the employer's conduct occurred.

Source read [www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_12](https://www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_12)

Must be raised in the first Rule 12 motion, or in the answer / Rule 15(a)(1) amendment (Rule 12(h)(1)).

Fed. R. Civ. P. 12(b)(4), 12(g)(2), 12(h)(1)

#### Motion to dismiss for insufficient process (Rule 12(b)(4))

DispositiveVerified

Attacks the CONTENT or FORM of the summons itself — wrong name, missing seal, defective document.

When to use it When the summons is technically defective on its face.

If you miss it WAIVED under Rule 12(h)(1).

Notes & source

Response / reply Set by local rule.

Pro se note 12(b)(4) is about the paper; 12(b)(5) is about the delivery. Courts often treat them interchangeably, but plead the right one.

Source read [www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_12](https://www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_12)

Must be raised in the first Rule 12 motion, or in the answer / Rule 15(a)(1) amendment (Rule 12(h)(1)).

Fed. R. Civ. P. 12(b)(5), 12(g)(2), 12(h)(1); Fed. R. Civ. P. 4(m)

#### Motion to dismiss for insufficient service of process (Rule 12(b)(5))

DispositiveVerified

Attacks the MANNER or TIMING of delivery — served on the wrong person, by an ineligible server, or outside the Rule 4(m) 90-day window.

When to use it When the summons and complaint were not delivered in a way Rule 4 permits.

If you miss it WAIVED under Rule 12(h)(1). For the plaintiff, a successful 12(b)(5) usually means dismissal without prejudice — which can be fatal if the limitations period has since run.

Notes & source

Response / reply Set by local rule.

Pro se note A pro se plaintiff cannot serve process personally. Use the U.S. Marshal (available on an IFP grant), a process server, or any non-party adult over 18.

Source read [www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_12](https://www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_12)

"A motion asserting any of these defenses must be made before pleading if a responsive pleading is allowed" (Rule 12(b)).

But the defense is NOT waived if omitted: under Rule 12(h)(2) it may still be raised in any Rule 7(a) pleading, by a Rule 12(c) motion, or at trial.

Fed. R. Civ. P. 12(b)(6), 12(h)(2)

#### Motion to dismiss for failure to state a claim (Rule 12(b)(6))

DispositiveVerified

Argues that even if everything in the complaint is true, it does not state a legally viable claim. The single most common dispositive motion filed against pro se complaints.

When to use it Before answering, when the complaint's allegations do not add up to a recognized cause of action.

If you miss it Dismissal, often with leave to amend the first time. Not waived if omitted from a first motion — but the court may still refuse a successive Rule 12 motion under Rule 12(g)(2).

Notes & source

Response / reply E.D. Mo. L.R. 4.01(B): memorandum in opposition within 14 days after service of the motion; 4.01(C): reply within 10 days after service of the opposition. W.D. Mo. L.R. 7.0: suggestions in opposition within 14 days; reply within 14 days.

Pro se note Answer a 12(b)(6) with facts, not adjectives. If the court dismisses, immediately ask for leave to amend under Rule 15(a)(2) — courts 'should freely give leave when justice so requires.'

Source read [www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_12](https://www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_12)

Not waived by omission from a first motion …

Rule 12(h)(2) allows failure to join a Rule 19(b) person to be raised in any Rule 7(a) pleading, by a Rule 12(c) motion, or at trial.

Fed. R. Civ. P. 12(b)(7), 12(h)(2); Fed. R. Civ. P. 19

#### Motion to dismiss for failure to join a required party under Rule 19 (Rule 12(b)(7))

DispositiveVerified

Argues an absent person is so essential that the case cannot fairly proceed without them, and that joining them is not feasible.

When to use it When a non-party's interests would be impaired by the judgment, or their absence exposes an existing party to inconsistent obligations.

If you miss it Rarely fatal on its own — the usual remedy is joinder under Rule 19(a)(2), not dismissal. Dismissal only follows the Rule 19(b) 'equity and good conscience' analysis.

Notes & source

Response / reply Set by local rule — E.D. Mo. L.R. 4.01(B)/(C); W.D. Mo. L.R. 7.0.

Source read [www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_12](https://www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_12)

Rule 12(h)(1): 12(b)(2)–(5) [personal jurisdiction …

venue, process, service] are WAIVED by omission from a Rule 12 motion, or by failing to make them by motion or include them in a responsive pleading or a Rule 15(a)(1) amendment as of course. Rule 12(h)(2): 12(b)(6) failure to state a claim, failure to join a Rule 19(b) party, and failure to state a legal defense may still be raised in a Rule 7(a) pleading, by a Rule 12(c) motion, or at trial. Rule 12(h)(3): subject-matter jurisdiction is never waived.

Fed. R. Civ. P. 12(g)(2), 12(h)(1), 12(h)(2), 12(h)(3)

#### Rule 12(g)(2) consolidation requirement and Rule 12(h) waiver map

ProceduralVerified

The trap that decides which defenses survive. Rule 12(g)(2): "a party that makes a motion under this rule must not make another motion under this rule raising a defense or objection that was available to the party but omitted from its earlier motion."

When to use it Read this BEFORE filing any Rule 12 motion — you generally get one bite.

If you miss it Permanent loss of the 12(b)(2)–(5) defenses.

Notes & source

Pro se note Three tiers, easy to remember: (2)-(3)-(4)-(5) use them or lose them; (6) and (7) survive until trial; (1) survives forever.

Source read [www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_12](https://www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_12)

"After the pleadings are closed—but early enough not to delay trial—a party may move for judgment on the pleadings" (Rule 12(c)). No fixed number of days.

Fed. R. Civ. P. 12(c); Fed. R. Civ. P. 12(d)

#### Motion for judgment on the pleadings (Rule 12(c))

DispositiveVerified

Asks the court to decide the case on the pleadings alone once they are closed. Judged by the same standard as a 12(b)(6) motion.

When to use it After the answer is filed, when the pleadings themselves show one side must win. Also the vehicle for a late 12(b)(6) or 12(b)(7) defense under Rule 12(h)(2)(B).

If you miss it No waiver, but a motion filed close to trial may be denied as untimely under the 'early enough not to delay trial' clause.

Notes & source

Response / reply E.D. Mo. L.R. 4.01(B)/(C): 14 days / 10 days. W.D. Mo. L.R. 7.0: 14 days / 14 days.

Pro se note Rule 12(d): if you attach outside evidence to a 12(b)(6) or 12(c) motion and the court doesn't exclude it, the motion converts to summary judgment and all parties get a reasonable opportunity to present Rule 56 material.

Source read [www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_12](https://www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_12)

"The motion must be made before filing a responsive pleading and must point out the defects complained of and the details desired" (Rule 12(e)).

If the court orders a more definite statement, it must be obeyed "within 14 days after notice of the order or within the time the court sets."

Fed. R. Civ. P. 12(e)

#### Motion for a more definite statement (Rule 12(e))

ProceduralVerified

Asks the court to order the other side to clarify a pleading that is "so vague or ambiguous that the party cannot reasonably prepare a response."

When to use it Rarely granted. Use only when a pleading is genuinely unintelligible — not merely thin.

If you miss it If a party ordered to replead does not do so in 14 days, "the court may strike the pleading or issue any other appropriate order."

Notes & source

Response / reply Set by local rule. The responsive pleading is then due 14 days after the more definite statement is served (Rule 12(a)(4)(B)).

Pro se note Also triggers Rule 15(a)(1)(B): service of a 12(e) motion starts the 21-day clock to amend once as of right.

Source read [www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_12](https://www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_12)

The court may act on its own; or "on motion made by a party either before responding to the pleading or …

if a response is not allowed, within 21 days after being served with the pleading" (Rule 12(f)(2)).

Fed. R. Civ. P. 12(f)

#### Motion to strike (Rule 12(f))

ProceduralVerified

Asks the court to strike "an insufficient defense or any redundant, immaterial, impertinent, or scandalous matter" from a pleading.

When to use it To remove a legally baseless affirmative defense, or genuinely scandalous allegations. Disfavored as a general cleanup tool.

If you miss it The 21-day window closes, though the court retains power to strike on its own under Rule 12(f)(1).

Notes & source

Response / reply E.D. Mo. L.R. 4.01(B)/(C): 14 days / 10 days. W.D. Mo. L.R. 7.0: 14 days / 14 days.

Pro se note An answer is a pleading to which no response is allowed, so the 21-day clock is what governs a motion to strike affirmative defenses.

Source read [www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_12](https://www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_12)

"21 days after serving it, or if the pleading is one to which a responsive pleading is required …

21 days after service of a responsive pleading or 21 days after service of a motion under Rule 12(b), (e), or (f), whichever is earlier" (Rule 15(a)(1)).

Fed. R. Civ. P. 15(a)(1)

#### Amended pleading as a matter of course (Rule 15(a)(1))

ProceduralVerified

A one-time right to amend a pleading without asking anyone's permission.

When to use it The single most valuable pro se tool after a motion to dismiss is filed — you can fix the complaint yourself instead of briefing the motion.

If you miss it You lose the free amendment and must obtain written consent or move for leave under Rule 15(a)(2).

Notes & source

Response / reply Response to an amended pleading is due "within the time remaining to respond to the original pleading or within 14 days after service of the amended pleading, whichever is later" (Rule 15(a)(3)).

Pro se note When a 12(b)(6) motion lands, you have 21 days to simply file a better complaint — which usually moots the motion. This is often stronger than opposing it.

Source read [www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_15](https://www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_15)

No fixed deadline in Rule 15. "The court should freely give leave when justice so requires." BUT once a Rule 16(b) …

scheduling order sets an amendment cutoff, that date controls and can be changed "only for good cause and with the judge's consent" (Rule 16(b)(4)).

Fed. R. Civ. P. 15(a)(2); Fed. R. Civ. P. 16(b)(3)(A), 16(b)(4)

#### Motion for leave to amend a pleading (Rule 15(a)(2))

ProceduralVerified

Asks the court's permission to amend after the free amendment window has closed.

When to use it Whenever new facts, new claims, or new parties emerge — or to cure a defect the court identified.

If you miss it After the scheduling-order cutoff, the liberal Rule 15 standard is replaced by the stricter Rule 16(b)(4) 'good cause' standard, which focuses on your diligence.

Notes & source

Response / reply E.D. Mo. L.R. 4.01(B)/(C): 14 days / 10 days. W.D. Mo. L.R. 7.0: 14 days / 14 days.

Pro se note Attach the proposed amended pleading to the motion. Many judges deny leave simply because the proposed pleading was not provided.

Source read [www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_15](https://www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_15)

No independent deadline. For changing the party named, the new party must have received notice "within the period provided by Rule 4(m) …

for serving the summons and complaint" (Rule 4(m) = 90 days after the complaint is filed), and must have known or should have known the action would have been brought against it but for a mistake in identity (Rule 15(c)(1)(C)).

Fed. R. Civ. P. 15(c)(1)(A)-(C); Fed. R. Civ. P. 4(m)

#### Relation back of an amendment (Rule 15(c))

ProceduralVerified

A doctrine, invoked within a motion for leave to amend, that treats a new claim or new defendant as if it had been filed on the date of the original complaint — defeating a limitations defense.

When to use it Critical when the EEOC 90-day window or the FMLA 2-/3-year period has run since you filed, and you need to add a claim or correct a defendant's name.

If you miss it Without relation back, the amended claim is time-barred and dismissed.

Notes & source

Pro se note Naming the wrong corporate entity as the employer is a classic pro se problem. Rule 15(c)(1)(C) is designed for exactly that — but only if the right entity got notice within 90 days of filing.

Source read [www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_15](https://www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_15)

No fixed deadline — "On motion and reasonable notice, the court may, on just terms, permit a party to serve a supplemental pleading."

Fed. R. Civ. P. 15(d)

#### Motion for leave to file a supplemental pleading (Rule 15(d))

ProceduralVerified

Adds events that happened AFTER the original pleading was filed — as opposed to an amendment, which revises what already existed.

When to use it In an employment case, when retaliation or further adverse action occurs after suit is filed.

If you miss it Post-filing events must be brought as a separate lawsuit, with its own exhaustion and limitations problems.

Notes & source

Response / reply Set by local rule; the court may order a response to the supplemental pleading.

Source read [www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_15](https://www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_15)

### Parties & joinder 5

"On timely motion" — Rule 24 sets NO number of days. Timeliness is judged by how far the case has progressed and prejudice to the parties.

Fed. R. Civ. P. 24(a)

#### Motion to intervene as of right (Rule 24(a))

ProceduralVerified

A non-party's motion to join an existing lawsuit because a statute gives an unconditional right, or because the non-party's interest may be practically impaired by the outcome.

When to use it When you are not a party but the judgment will affect your interest and no existing party adequately represents it.

If you miss it Denial as untimely. Courts get markedly stricter after discovery closes or judgment enters.

Notes & source

Response / reply Set by local rule — E.D. Mo. L.R. 4.01(B)/(C); W.D. Mo. L.R. 7.0.

Pro se note Rule 24(c): the motion "must state the grounds for intervention and be accompanied by a pleading that sets out the claim or defense for which intervention is sought." Omitting that pleading is a common ground for denial.

Source read [www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_24](https://www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_24)

"On timely motion" — no fixed number of days.

Rule 24(b)(3): the court "must consider whether the intervention will unduly delay or prejudice the adjudication of the original parties' rights."

Fed. R. Civ. P. 24(b), 24(b)(3), 24(c)

#### Motion for permissive intervention (Rule 24(b))

ProceduralVerified

Discretionary intervention where the movant has a conditional statutory right, or a claim or defense sharing a common question of law or fact with the main action.

When to use it When you cannot meet the impairment test of Rule 24(a) but share a common question.

If you miss it Denial; you must file your own action.

Notes & source

Response / reply Set by local rule.

Source read [www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_24](https://www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_24)

No fixed deadline. Rule 19(a)(2): "If a person has not been joined as required, the court must order that the person be made a party." Rule 19(c) …

requires a pleading party to state the name of any required-but-unjoined person and the reasons for not joining them.

Fed. R. Civ. P. 19(a), 19(b), 19(c)

#### Motion to join a required party (Rule 19)

ProceduralVerified

Asks the court to order joinder of an absent person needed for complete relief, or whose absence would impair their interest or expose a party to inconsistent obligations.

When to use it When a necessary person is missing and joinder is feasible.

If you miss it If joinder is not feasible, the court applies the four Rule 19(b) factors to decide whether the case must be dismissed.

Notes & source

Response / reply Set by local rule.

Source read [www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_19](https://www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_19)

No fixed deadline in Rule 20. In practice governed by the Rule 16(b)(3)(A) scheduling-order cutoff for joining additional parties.

Fed. R. Civ. P. 20(a)(1), 20(a)(2), 20(a)(3), 20(b)

#### Motion for permissive joinder of parties (Rule 20)

ProceduralVerified

Allows multiple plaintiffs or defendants in one action where the claims arise from the same transaction, occurrence, or series, and share a common question of law or fact.

When to use it To sue related corporate entities or add co-plaintiffs with parallel claims. Usually accomplished through an amended pleading rather than a stand-alone motion.

If you miss it After the Rule 16 cutoff you must show good cause under Rule 16(b)(4).

Notes & source

Response / reply Set by local rule.

Pro se note Rule 20(b) lets the court order separate trials to protect a party from embarrassment, delay, expense, or other prejudice.

Source read [www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_20](https://www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_20)

"On motion or on its own, the court may at any time, on just terms, add or drop a party. The court may also sever any claim against a party." No deadline.

Fed. R. Civ. P. 21

#### Motion to add or drop a party, or to sever a claim (Rule 21)

ProceduralVerified

The cure for misjoinder, and the mechanism for splitting a claim against one party into its own action.

When to use it To drop an improperly named defendant, or to sever claims that should be tried separately.

If you miss it Nothing — "Misjoinder of parties is not a ground for dismissing an action."

Notes & source

Response / reply Set by local rule.

Pro se note If a defendant moves to dismiss the whole case because one party is misjoined, Rule 21's first sentence is your answer.

Source read [www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_21](https://www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_21)

### Default 3

No deadline. Triggered when "a party against whom a judgment for affirmative relief is sought has failed to plead or otherwise defend …

and that failure is shown by affidavit or otherwise" (Rule 55(a)).

Fed. R. Civ. P. 55(a)

#### Application for entry of default (Rule 55(a))

ProceduralVerified

A request to the CLERK (not the judge) to note on the docket that a defendant has failed to plead or otherwise defend. It is a prerequisite to a default judgment.

When to use it After the defendant's answer deadline has passed with no answer and no Rule 12 motion.

If you miss it No penalty for delay, but courts disfavor default and a defendant who appears late will usually be allowed in.

Notes & source

Pro se note Entry of default is a two-step process. Get the clerk's entry under 55(a) FIRST; a motion for default judgment filed without it is routinely denied.

Source read [www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_55](https://www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_55)

No deadline. But if the defaulting party "has appeared personally or by a representative …

that party or its representative must be served with written notice of the application at least 7 days before the hearing" (Rule 55(b)(2)).

Fed. R. Civ. P. 55(b)(1), 55(b)(2), 55(d)

#### Motion for default judgment (Rule 55(b))

DispositiveVerified

Asks for judgment against a defaulted party. The clerk may enter it if the claim is for a sum certain (55(b)(1)); otherwise the court must (55(b)(2)).

When to use it After the clerk has entered default under Rule 55(a).

If you miss it Failure to give the 7-day notice is itself grounds to set the judgment aside.

Notes & source

Response / reply 7 days' written notice to an appearing defaulted party before the hearing (Rule 55(b)(2)).

Pro se note Employment damages are not a 'sum certain,' so expect a Rule 55(b)(2) hearing on damages rather than a clerk's judgment.

Source read [www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_55](https://www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_55)

Entry of default: no deadline — "The court may set aside an entry of default for good cause." Default JUDGMENT …

governed by Rule 60(b), so "within a reasonable time" and, for reasons (1), (2), and (3), "no more than a year after the entry of the judgment" (Rule 60(c)(1)).

Fed. R. Civ. P. 55(c); Fed. R. Civ. P. 60(b), 60(c)(1)

#### Motion to set aside default or default judgment (Rule 55(c))

ProceduralVerified
