Source read [www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frap/rule_4](https://www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frap/rule_4)

60 days after entry of the judgment or order appealed from (FRAP 4(a)(1)(B)).

Fed. R. App. P. 4(a)(1)(B)

#### Notice of appeal — 60 days when the United States is a party (FRAP 4(a)(1)(B))

AppellateVerified

A doubled appeal window when a federal party is involved.

When to use it When the United States, a U.S. agency, or a U.S. officer or employee sued in an official capacity (or in an individual capacity for acts connected with federal duties) is a party.

If you miss it Same jurisdictional bar as the 30-day rule.

Notes & source

Pro se note A private employer case gets 30 days, not 60. Do not assume the longer period applies just because a federal statute (FMLA/ADA) is involved — it turns on who the PARTIES are.

Source read [www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frap/rule_4](https://www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frap/rule_4)

If a party timely files a motion under Civil Rule 50(b) …

52(b), 54 (for attorney's fees, if the court so orders under Rule 58), 59 (new trial or to alter/amend), or 60 (IF filed no later than 28 days after entry of judgment), then "the time to file an appeal runs for all parties from the entry of the order disposing of the last such remaining motion" (FRAP 4(a)(4)(A)).

Fed. R. App. P. 4(a)(4)(A), 4(a)(4)(B)

#### Effect of post-judgment motions on the appeal clock (FRAP 4(a)(4))

AppellateVerified

Certain timely post-judgment motions suspend the notice-of-appeal deadline and restart it from scratch.

When to use it Whenever you file a post-judgment motion and need to know when the appeal clock actually runs.

If you miss it If the underlying motion is UNTIMELY, it does not toll anything, and the original 30-day clock keeps running. This is how litigants lose an appeal while thinking they preserved it.

Notes & source

Pro se note Note the asymmetry: a Rule 60 motion tolls the appeal clock only if filed within 28 days of judgment — the same 28 days as Rule 59(e). Outside that, a Rule 60(b) motion does not extend your time to appeal.

Source read [www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frap/rule_4](https://www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frap/rule_4)

The district court may extend only if "a party so moves no later than 30 days after the time prescribed by this Rule 4(a) …

expires" and "that party shows excusable neglect or good cause." The extension may not exceed 30 days after the prescribed time or 14 days after the order granting the motion, whichever is later.

Fed. R. App. P. 4(a)(5)(A), 4(a)(5)(C)

#### Motion to extend the time to file a notice of appeal (FRAP 4(a)(5))

AppellateNo extensionVerified

The only sanctioned way to file a late notice of appeal, decided by the DISTRICT court.

When to use it When the 30- or 60-day window has just closed and you have a genuine excuse.

If you miss it Once that additional 30 days passes, no extension is possible and the appeal right is gone.

Notes & source

Response / reply A motion filed after the original deadline expires must be served on the other parties (FRAP 4(a)(5)(B)).

Pro se note File it in the DISTRICT court. Effectively you have a hard outer limit of 60 days from judgment in an ordinary civil case — 30 to appeal plus 30 to ask for more time.

Source read [www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frap/rule_4](https://www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frap/rule_4)

The district court may reopen for 14 days only if ALL of these are met …

(A) the court finds the moving party did not receive Civil Rule 77(d) notice of the entry within 21 days after entry; (B) the motion is filed "within 180 days after the judgment or order is entered or within 14 days after the moving party receives notice under Federal Rule of Civil Procedure 77(d) of the entry, whichever is earlier"; and (C) no party would be prejudiced.

Fed. R. App. P. 4(a)(6)

#### Motion to reopen the time to file an appeal (FRAP 4(a)(6))

AppellateVerified

A narrow rescue when you never received notice that judgment was entered.

When to use it When the clerk's notice of entry never reached you and the appeal deadline passed unknowingly.

If you miss it No reopening; the appeal is permanently lost.

Notes & source

Pro se note Keep your mailing address current with the district clerk. If you move and never get the judgment, this rule is your only remedy — and its 14-day/180-day limits are both hard.

Source read [www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frap/rule_4](https://www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frap/rule_4)

"[A] defendant's notice of appeal must be filed in the district court within 14 days after the later of: (i) the entry of either the judgment or the order being appealed;

or (ii) the filing of the government's notice of appeal" (FRAP 4(b)(1)(A)). The government's notice: 30 days (FRAP 4(b)(1)(B)).

Fed. R. App. P. 4(b)(1)(A), 4(b)(1)(B), 4(b)(3)(A), 4(b)(4)

#### Notice of appeal — criminal case (FRAP 4(b))

AppellateVerified

The criminal defendant's notice of appeal, on a much shorter clock than civil.

When to use it After entry of the judgment of conviction or the order being appealed.

If you miss it Unlike the civil deadline, FRAP 4(b) is a claim-processing rule rather than jurisdictional in most circuits — but the government can and does enforce it, and the appeal will be dismissed if it objects.

Notes & source

Pro se note FRAP 4(b)(3)(A): a timely Criminal Rule 29, 33, or 34 motion restarts the 14 days from entry of the order disposing of the last such motion. FRAP 4(b)(4): the district court may extend up to 30 days from the expiration of the time, on a finding of excusable neglect or good cause.

Source read [www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frap/rule_4](https://www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frap/rule_4)

### Appellate deadlines 1

"For good cause, the court may extend the time prescribed by these rules or by its order to perform any act, or may permit an act to be done after that time expires.

But the court may not extend the time to file: (1) a notice of appeal (except as authorized in Rule 4) or a petition for permission to appeal; or (2) a notice of appeal from or a petition to enjoin, set aside, suspend, modify, enforce, or otherwise review an order of an administrative agency, board, commission, or officer of the United States, unless specifically authorized by law."

Fed. R. App. P. 26(b)(1), 26(b)(2)

#### Appellate deadlines that cannot be extended (FRAP 26(b))

Time computationNo extensionVerified

The short list of appellate deadlines beyond the court's power to enlarge.

When to use it Before relying on any extension in the court of appeals.

If you miss it The court of appeals has no authority to forgive a late notice of appeal except through FRAP 4(a)(5) extension or 4(a)(6) reopening in the district court.

Notes & source

Pro se note Everything else on appeal — briefs, motions, rehearing petitions — CAN be extended for good cause. The notice of appeal cannot.

Source read [www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frap/rule_26](https://www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frap/rule_26)

### Briefing 1

FRAP 31(a)(1) default: appellant "must serve and file a brief within 40 days after the record is filed";

appellee within 30 days after the appellant's brief is served; reply "within 21 days after service of the appellee's brief but a reply brief must be filed at least 7 days before argument, unless the court, for good cause, allows a later filing." These may be shortened or extended by local rule or court order. In the Eighth Circuit, 8th Cir. R. 11A states the appellant's brief is due 40 days after filing of the docket entries, and briefs are governed by the court's briefing schedule order (8th Cir. R. 31A is abrogated).

Fed. R. App. P. 31(a)(1), 31(b), 31(c); 8th Cir. R. 11A; 8th Cir. R. 28A

#### Briefing schedule (FRAP 31; 8th Cir. R. 11A, 28A)

AppellateVerified

The deadlines for the opening brief, response brief, and reply brief.

When to use it After the record is filed and the appeal is docketed.

If you miss it FRAP 31(c): if an appellant fails to file a brief on time, "an appellee may move to dismiss the appeal." An appellee who fails to file "will not be heard at oral argument unless the court grants permission."

Notes & source

Response / reply 40 / 30 / 21 days as above.

Pro se note Follow the Eighth Circuit's briefing schedule ORDER, not the FRAP default. 8th Cir. R. 28A(c): pro se litigants must submit one paper copy of their merits briefs by the date set in the court's briefing schedule. Motions for leave to file overlength briefs "will be granted only in extraordinary cases."

Source read [www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frap/rule_31](https://www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frap/rule_31)

### Appellate motions 3

No number of days. FRAP 8(a)(1): a party "must ordinarily move first in the district court" for a stay …

approval of a bond or other security, or an order suspending, modifying, restoring, or granting an injunction. FRAP 8(a)(2): a motion in the court of appeals must show "that moving first in the district court would be impracticable" or that the district court denied the motion or failed to afford the relief requested, and must state the reasons the district court gave.

Fed. R. App. P. 8(a)(1), 8(a)(2), 8(b), 8(c); Fed. R. Civ. P. 62

#### Motion for stay or injunction pending appeal (FRAP 8)

Emergency / injunctiveVerified

Asks to suspend a judgment or obtain an injunction while the appeal is pending.

When to use it When enforcement during the appeal would cause irreparable harm.

If you miss it The judgment or injunction is enforced during the appeal.

Notes & source

Response / reply Motion practice per FRAP 27: response within 10 days after service; reply within 7 days after service of the response.

Pro se note Going straight to the Eighth Circuit without first asking the district judge is the most common reason these are denied. Ask below first, and attach the district court's ruling.

Source read [www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frap/rule_8](https://www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frap/rule_8)

No fixed deadline for the initial motion …

which is filed in the DISTRICT court with an affidavit showing inability to pay, claiming entitlement to redress, and stating the issues intended to be presented. If the district court denies it, a motion in the court of appeals must be filed "within 30 days after service of the notice" of the district court's denial (FRAP 24(a)(5)).

Fed. R. App. P. 24(a)(1), 24(a)(2), 24(a)(3), 24(a)(4), 24(a)(5), 24(c)

#### Motion for leave to proceed in forma pauperis on appeal (FRAP 24)

AppellateVerified

Permission to appeal without prepaying the appellate docketing fee.

When to use it At the time of filing the notice of appeal.

If you miss it You must pay the appellate fees or the appeal is not processed.

Notes & source

Response / reply 30 days to renew in the court of appeals after notice of the district court's denial.

Pro se note FRAP 24(a)(3): if you were already granted IFP in the district court, you may proceed on appeal WITHOUT further authorization — unless the district court certifies the appeal is not taken in good faith or finds you are otherwise not entitled, and states its reasons in writing.

Source read [www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frap/rule_24](https://www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frap/rule_24)

Response: "any party may file a response to a motion" within 10 days after service of the motion, unless the court shortens or extends the time (FRAP 27(a)(3)(A)).

Reply: within 7 days after service of the response (FRAP 27(a)(4)).

Fed. R. App. P. 27(a)(1), 27(a)(2), 27(a)(3)(A), 27(a)(4), 27(c), 27(d)(2)

#### Motions in the court of appeals (FRAP 27)

AppellateVerified

The general motions rule on appeal. All legal argument goes in the motion itself; separate briefs in support are not permitted.

When to use it For any relief sought from the court of appeals.

If you miss it The motion is decided without your response.

Notes & source

Response / reply 10 days to respond; 7 days to reply.

Pro se note Length limits under FRAP 27(d)(2): a computer-generated motion or response may not exceed 5,200 words; a reply 2,600 words. Handwritten or typewritten: 20 pages and 10 pages. FRAP 27(c): a single circuit judge may act on most motions but "may not dismiss or otherwise determine an appeal or other proceeding."

Source read [www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frap/rule_27](https://www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frap/rule_27)

### After the decision 2

"[A]ny petition for panel rehearing or rehearing en banc must be filed within 14 days after judgment is entered." In a civil case in which the United States (or a U.S.

agency or officer) is a party, "the petition may be filed by any party within 45 days after entry of judgment."

Fed. R. App. P. 40 (as amended eff. Dec. 1, 2024); former Fed. R. App. P. 35 (abrogated, contents transferred to Rule 40); 8th Cir. R. 40A

#### Petition for panel rehearing or rehearing en banc (FRAP 40)

AppellateVerified

Asks the panel to reconsider its decision, or the full court to rehear the case. Effective December 1, 2024, former FRAP 35 was abrogated and its contents transferred into Rule 40, now titled 'Panel Rehearing; En Banc Determination.'

When to use it After an adverse decision from the panel, before the mandate issues.

If you miss it The panel decision stands and the mandate issues; the only remaining step is a petition for certiorari to the Supreme Court.

Notes & source

Response / reply "Unless the court so requests, no response to the petition is permitted. Ordinarily, the petition will not be granted without such a request."

Pro se note A petition for panel rehearing "must state with particularity each point of law or fact that the petitioner believes the court has overlooked or misapprehended." An en banc petition must begin with a statement that the decision conflicts with a decision of the Supreme Court or of the same court, or that the proceeding involves one or more questions of exceptional importance. 8th Cir. R. 40A: "Successive petitions for rehearing are not allowed."

Source read [www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frap/rule_40](https://www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frap/rule_40)

No deadline. Before the appeal is docketed in the court of appeals …

the DISTRICT court may dismiss it on the filing of a stipulation signed by all parties or on the appellant's motion with notice to all parties (FRAP 42(a)). After docketing: "The circuit clerk must dismiss a docketed appeal if the parties file a signed dismissal agreement specifying how costs are to be paid and pay any court fees that are due" (FRAP 42(b)(1)); otherwise on the appellant's motion on terms agreed by the parties or fixed by the court (FRAP 42(b)(2)).

Fed. R. App. P. 42(a), 42(b)(1), 42(b)(2), 42(b)(3), 42(c)

#### Motion for voluntary dismissal of an appeal (FRAP 42)

AppellateVerified

Ends the appeal at the appellant's request or by agreement.

When to use it On settlement, or when you decide not to pursue the appeal.

If you miss it Nothing; but relief beyond mere dismissal — such as vacating the district court's decision or remanding — requires a separate court order (FRAP 42(b)(3)).

Notes & source

Source read [www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frap/rule_42](https://www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frap/rule_42)

### U.S. Supreme Court review 3

'A petition for a writ of certiorari ... is timely when it is filed with the Clerk of this Court within 90 days after entry of the judgment' (Rule 13.1).

The 90 days run 'from the date of entry of the judgment or order sought to be reviewed, and not from the issuance date of the mandate'; if a petition for rehearing is timely filed in the lower court, the time runs 'from the date of the denial of rehearing or, if rehearing is granted, the subsequent entry of judgment' (Rule 13.3). In civil cases the 90 days is also statutory: 28 U.S.C. § 2101(c).

Sup. Ct. R. 13.1, 13.3 (adopted Feb. 17, 2026, eff. Mar. 16, 2026); 28 U.S.C. § 2101(c)

#### Petition for a writ of certiorari

AppellateVerified

The request that the Supreme Court review a final judgment of a federal court of appeals or a state court of last resort. Review is discretionary; the Court grants a small fraction of petitions.

When to use it After the court of appeals (for you, the Eighth Circuit) or the Missouri Supreme Court has entered judgment and any rehearing petition has been decided, if a federal question remains that is worth the Court's attention.

If you miss it In a civil case the 90-day period comes from § 2101(c) and the Court treats it as jurisdictional — a late petition is dismissed and the most a Justice can add is the 60 days in Rule 13.5. Count from entry of judgment, not from the mandate.

Notes & source

Response / reply A brief in opposition is due 30 days after the case is placed on the docket (Rule 15.3) — read Rule 15 before relying on it; only Rules 13 and 30 were read for this entry.

Pro se note Filing a rehearing petition in the court of appeals restarts the 90 days (Rule 13.3), which is often the only realistic way to buy time. Rule 39 lets a party proceed in forma pauperis on a motion and affidavit.

Source read [www.supremecourt.gov/filingandrules/2026RulesoftheCourt_WEB.pdf](https://www.supremecourt.gov/filingandrules/2026RulesoftheCourt_WEB.pdf)

'For good cause, a Justice may extend the time to file a petition for a writ of certiorari for a period not exceeding 60 days ...

The application must be filed with the Clerk at least 10 days before the date the petition is due' (Rule 13.5). Section 2101(c) sets the same 60-day ceiling for civil cases.

Sup. Ct. R. 13.5; 28 U.S.C. § 2101(c)

#### Application to extend the time to file a certiorari petition

AppellateNo extensionVerified

A single-Justice application for more time to file the petition, addressed to the Circuit Justice for the court whose judgment is being challenged.

When to use it When the 90 days will not be enough — but it must be filed before the petition is due, not after.

If you miss it An application filed inside the last 10 days is out of time under the rule's own terms, and no extension can run past day 150.

Notes & source

Pro se note Extensions are 'not favored' in the Court's practice — state a concrete reason, not simply that more time would help.

Source read [www.supremecourt.gov/filingandrules/2026RulesoftheCourt_WEB.pdf](https://www.supremecourt.gov/filingandrules/2026RulesoftheCourt_WEB.pdf)

'In the computation of any period of time prescribed or allowed by these Rules …

by order of the Court, or by an applicable statute, the day of the act, event, or default from which the designated period begins to run is not included' (Rule 30.1).

Sup. Ct. R. 30.1

#### Counting days under the Supreme Court Rules

Time computationVerified

The Court's own computation rule, which differs slightly from the district-court and appellate rules.

When to use it Whenever you compute a Supreme Court deadline — the 90-day certiorari period above included.

If you miss it Miscounting by one day at the front end is the classic error — day one is the day after judgment is entered.

Notes & source

Source read [www.supremecourt.gov/filingandrules/2026RulesoftheCourt_WEB.pdf](https://www.supremecourt.gov/filingandrules/2026RulesoftheCourt_WEB.pdf)

### Time computation 1

FRAP 26(a)(1): exclude the triggering day;

"count every day, including intermediate Saturdays, Sundays, and legal holidays"; include the last day unless it is a Saturday, Sunday, or legal holiday, in which case the period runs to the end of the next day that is not. If the clerk's office is inaccessible on the last day, the time extends to the first accessible day that is not a Saturday, Sunday, or legal holiday. FRAP 26(c): three days are added when a paper is served by means other than electronic (the 2016 amendment removed electronic service).

Fed. R. App. P. 26(a)(1), 26(a)(4), 26(a)(6), 26(c)

#### Computing and extending time — appellate (FRAP 26)

Time computationVerified

The counting rules in the courts of appeals.

When to use it Every appellate deadline.

If you miss it A miscounted notice of appeal is unfixable — see FRAP 26(b)(1).

Notes & source

Pro se note FRAP 26(a)(6) defines 'legal holiday' to include the federal holidays (New Year's Day, Martin Luther King Jr.'s Birthday, Washington's Birthday, Memorial Day, Juneteenth, Independence Day, Labor Day, Columbus Day, Veterans' Day, Thanksgiving, Christmas), any day declared a holiday by the President or Congress, and — for periods counted forward — state holidays where the district or circuit clerk's office is located.

Source read [www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frap/rule_26](https://www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frap/rule_26)

## Clocks that decide the case — EEOC, MHRA, FMLA 6

### Administrative exhaustion (EEOC / MCHR) 2

"A charge under this section shall be filed within one hundred and eighty days after the alleged unlawful employment practice occurred...

except that in a case of an unlawful employment practice with respect to which the person aggrieved has initially instituted proceedings with a State or local agency with authority to grant or seek relief from such practice..., such charge shall be filed by or on behalf of the person aggrieved within three hundred days after the alleged unlawful employment practice occurred, or within thirty days after receiving notice that the State or local agency has terminated the proceedings under the State or local law, whichever is earlier" (42 U.S.C. § 2000e-5(e)(1)).

42 U.S.C. § 2000e-5(e)(1); applied to the ADA by 42 U.S.C. § 12117(a)

#### EEOC charge filing deadline — 300 days in Missouri (deferral state)

Limitations clockVerified

The administrative charge is a precondition to an ADA or Title VII lawsuit. The base period is 180 days, extended to 300 days where the complainant first instituted proceedings with a state or local fair-employment agency.

When to use it Immediately after an adverse employment action based on disability or another protected characteristic.

If you miss it The claim is administratively unexhausted and will be dismissed.

Notes & source

Pro se note Missouri is a deferral state because the Missouri Commission on Human Rights is a FEPA with a worksharing agreement with the EEOC — which is what makes the 300-day period available. Do not rely on 300 days without confirming the dual-filing occurred; the safe course is to file within 180 days.

Source read [www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/42/2000e-5](https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/42/2000e-5)

"[A]ny person claiming to be aggrieved by an unlawful discriminatory practice shall make …

sign and file with the commission a verified complaint in writing, within one hundred eighty days of the alleged act of discrimination" (RSMo § 213.075(1)).

Mo. Rev. Stat. § 213.075(1)

#### Missouri Human Rights Act — MCHR charge within 180 days

Limitations clockVerified

The state administrative charge that precedes an MHRA lawsuit.

When to use it For a state-law discrimination claim in Missouri, parallel to the EEOC charge.

If you miss it The MHRA claim is barred. Note this 180-day state deadline is SHORTER than the 300-day federal deadline.

Notes & source

Pro se note A charge dual-filed with the EEOC is normally cross-filed with the MCHR, but confirm the cross-filing in writing rather than assuming it. The 180-day state clock is the tighter one — calendar to it.

Source read [revisor.mo.gov/main/OneSection.aspx?section=213.075](https://revisor.mo.gov/main/OneSection.aspx?section=213.075)

### Filing suit 4

Within ninety days after the giving of such notice a civil action may be brought against the respondent (42 U.S.C. § 2000e-5(f)(1)).

42 U.S.C. § 2000e-5(f)(1); applied to the ADA by 42 U.S.C. § 12117(a)

#### 90-day right-to-sue window

Limitations clockVerified

The window to file a federal lawsuit after the EEOC issues a notice of right to sue.

When to use it The moment the right-to-sue notice arrives.

If you miss it The suit is time-barred. This is a short, strictly enforced period, subject only to narrow equitable tolling.

Notes & source

Pro se note 90 days is not three months. Courts generally measure from RECEIPT of the notice, and some presume receipt a few days after the mailing date. Docket the deadline from the mailing date to be safe, and file the complaint even if it is imperfect — you can amend under Rule 15.

Source read [www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/42/2000e-5](https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/42/2000e-5)

"The powers, remedies, and procedures set forth in sections 2000e-4 …

2000e-5, 2000e-6, 2000e-8, and 2000e-9 of this title shall be the powers, remedies, and procedures this subchapter provides to the Commission, to the Attorney General, or to any person alleging discrimination on the basis of disability in violation of any provision of this chapter... concerning employment" (42 U.S.C. § 12117(a)). So the 180/300-day charge deadline and the 90-day suit deadline both apply to ADA employment claims.

42 U.S.C. § 12117(a); 42 U.S.C. § 2000e-5(e)(1), (f)(1)

#### ADA employment claims incorporate Title VII procedures

Limitations clockVerified

The ADA does not have its own charge-filing and right-to-sue machinery; it borrows Title VII's.

When to use it Whenever calculating deadlines for an ADA Title I employment claim.

If you miss it Same as Title VII: unexhausted or untimely claims are dismissed.

Notes & source

Pro se note Note the contrast with FMLA: the FMLA requires NO administrative charge at all, and has its own limitations period. Mixing the two clocks is a common and costly error.

Source read [www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/42/12117](https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/42/12117)

"[A]n action may be brought under this section not later than 2 years after the date of the last event constituting the alleged violation for which the action …

is brought." "In the case of such action brought for a willful violation of section 2615 of this title, such action may be brought within 3 years of the date of the last event constituting the alleged violation for which such action is brought" (29 U.S.C. § 2617(c)(1), (c)(2)).

29 U.S.C. § 2617(c)(1), 29 U.S.C. § 2617(c)(2); see 29 U.S.C. § 2617(a)(1), (a)(2)

#### FMLA limitations period — 2 years, or 3 years if willful

Limitations clockVerified

The statute of limitations for a private FMLA action. No EEOC charge or administrative exhaustion is required.

When to use it For interference or retaliation claims under 29 U.S.C. § 2615.

If you miss it The FMLA claim is time-barred.

Notes & source

Pro se note The FMLA clock runs from the LAST EVENT constituting the violation — not from when you discovered it. Because there is no exhaustion requirement, an FMLA claim can be filed immediately, even while an EEOC charge on the ADA claim is still pending.

Where the commission has not completed its administrative processing within 180 days and the aggrieved party requests in writing …

the commission issues a letter "indicating his or her right to bring a civil action within ninety days." Any action "shall be filed within ninety days from the date of the commission's notification letter to the individual but no later than two years after the alleged cause occurred or its reasonable discovery by the alleged injured party" (RSMo § 213.111(1)).

#### Missouri Human Rights Act — 90 days from right-to-sue, and 2-year outer limit

Limitations clockVerified

The window to file an MHRA civil action after the Commission's notification letter, plus an absolute two-year backstop.

When to use it After receiving the MCHR right-to-sue letter.

If you miss it The MHRA suit is barred. BOTH limits apply — the 90 days from the letter AND the two-year outer limit measured from the act or its reasonable discovery.

Notes & source
