## Is a UCC “charge” civil or criminal?

**Short answer.** The Uniform Commercial Code is civil law — Missouri's copy is RSMo Chapter 400. It creates private rights between parties to commercial deals (sales, leases, checks, secured loans), enforced by lawsuits for money or property. It creates no crimes, and there is no such thing as “filing UCC charges” against someone: only a prosecutor files charges. What people call a “UCC filing” is a UCC-1 financing statement — a public notice that a debtor has *already granted* a creditor a security interest in collateral. Filing one against a person who never signed a security agreement with you does not create a debt or a lien, and in Missouri it is a felony under RSMo 570.095 (class C if the target is a judge, official, officer or their family). The full explanation, with the statutes, follows.

**Prepared for:** Tyler Garner (pro se, Missouri)
**Question asked:** *"If you file UCC charges against someone, is it still civil or criminal, or is there something special about the UCC?"*
**Date of research:** September 9, 2026
**All citations below were read from primary sources this session.** See "Sources Actually Read" at the end.

> **This is legal information, not legal advice.** I am not your lawyer. Statutes get amended and courts interpret them in ways a summary can't capture. Before you act on anything here — especially anything about your Jeep claim's deadline — confirm it against the current statute and, if you can, run it past a Missouri attorney. Several free/low-cost options exist through Legal Services of Eastern Missouri, Legal Aid of Western Missouri, and the Missouri Bar's lawyer referral service.

---

### The Short Answer First

**The UCC is civil law. Entirely.** There is no such thing as "filing UCC charges" against someone.

Three things are getting mixed together in that phrase, and pulling them apart is most of the answer:

| The phrase | What it actually means |
| --- | --- |
| **"Charges"** | A word that belongs to *criminal* law. Only a prosecutor (a State's Attorney, Circuit Attorney, or U.S. Attorney) files charges. A private person cannot file criminal charges against anyone — you can report a crime, but the decision to charge is the government's alone. |
| **"Claims"** | What a private person files. You file a *petition* in civil court stating *claims* (conversion, breach of contract, fraud). This is what you're actually doing in your Jeep case. |
| **"A UCC filing"** | Something else entirely — not a court document at all. It's a **UCC-1 financing statement**, an administrative notice filed with the Secretary of State's office to tell the world that a debtor already gave a creditor a security interest in specific collateral. It is not a lawsuit, not a judgment, and not a charge. |

So: the UCC gives you **civil claims and civil remedies** — money damages, replevin (getting property back), foreclosing on collateral. It creates **no crimes**, and nobody "charges" anyone under it.

**And here's the part that matters most:** filing a UCC-1 against a person who never gave you a security interest is not a way to pressure them, put a lien on them, or "charge" them. In Missouri, doing that knowingly is itself **a felony** under RSMo 570.095, and if the target is a judge, prosecutor, or police officer it's a **more serious felony** — and potentially a **federal** crime carrying up to 10 years under 18 U.S.C. § 1521. Section 3 below covers this in detail. I'm flagging it up front because it's the single most consequential thing in this document.

---

### 1. What the UCC Actually Is

#### It's a model code, not a federal law

The **Uniform Commercial Code** is a *model* statute — a template drafted jointly by two private law-reform organizations, the **American Law Institute (ALI)** and the **Uniform Law Commission (ULC**, formerly the National Conference of Commissioners on Uniform State Laws). It is not itself law anywhere.

It becomes law only when a **state legislature enacts it**. Every state has enacted some version, which is why commercial law looks broadly similar from state to state — but each state's version is its own state statute, with its own section numbers and its own local variations.

There is a common misconception that the UCC is a federal or international body of law that operates above state courts. It isn't. When you're in a Missouri court, the UCC that applies is **Missouri's statute**, and it's interpreted by Missouri courts.

#### Missouri's enactment: RSMo Chapter 400

Missouri enacted the UCC as **Chapter 400 of the Revised Statutes of Missouri**, titled **"Uniform Commercial Code"**, located in Title XXVI ("Trade and Commerce"). Chapter 400 contains these articles (verified from the chapter index at revisor.mo.gov):

| Article | Subject | Missouri sections begin at |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Article 1** | General Provisions | § 400.1-101 |
| **Article 2** | Sales | § 400.2-101 |
| **Article 2A** | Leases | § 400.2A-101 |
| **Article 3** | Negotiable Instruments | § 400.3-101 |
| **Article 4** | Bank Deposits and Collections | § 400.4-101 |
| **Article 4A** | Funds Transfers | § 400.4A-101 |
| **Article 5** | Letters of Credit | § 400.5-101 |
| **Article 7** | Documents of Title | § 400.7-101 |
| **Article 8** | Investment Securities | § 400.8-101 |
| **Article 9** | Secured Transactions | § 400.9-101 |

(There's no Article 6 in force — the old Article 6 on bulk transfers has been repealed, though § 400.2-403(4) still contains a leftover cross-reference to it.)

Notice the numbering convention: Missouri prefixes the standard UCC article/section number with **"400."**. So UCC § 2-725 is **RSMo 400.2-725**; UCC § 9-203 is **RSMo 400.9-203**. When you cite it in a Missouri filing, use the Missouri number.

#### It governs private commercial transactions — and it is civil

Chapter 400 sets the ground rules for **private dealings between private parties**: who owes what when a sale of goods goes wrong, when title to goods passes, what a bank must do with a check, how a lender perfects a security interest in collateral.

Every remedy in it is a **civil** remedy, obtained by one private party suing another:

- **Money damages** — e.g., a buyer's remedies under § 400.2-711 ("Buyer's remedies in general"), which lets a buyer cancel, recover the price paid, and "cover" (buy substitute goods and recover the difference) under § 400.2-712.
- **Damages for a secured party's misconduct** — § 400.9-625(b) makes a person "liable for damages in the amount of any loss caused by a failure to comply with this article."
- **Statutory damages** — § 400.9-625(e) allows recovery of "five hundred dollars in each case" for specific violations.
- **Court orders restraining enforcement** — § 400.9-625(a): "a court may order or restrain collection, enforcement, or disposition of collateral on appropriate terms and conditions."
- **Repossession and foreclosure of collateral** — § 400.9-609, § 400.9-610.

**Nothing in Chapter 400 defines a crime or authorizes anyone to be arrested, fined, or jailed.** Missouri's crimes live in Title XXXVIII (Chapters 556–579) — an entirely different part of the statute books. Section 5 below shows where the commercial-paper *crimes* actually are.

#### Why the vocabulary confusion happens

The word "file" does double duty, and that's the trap:

- You **file a petition** in a *court* → that starts a **lawsuit**. A judge decides it. It can result in a judgment against someone.
- You **file a UCC-1** with the *Secretary of State* → that's a **clerical notice entry** in a searchable index. No judge sees it. No one evaluates whether it's true. A clerk indexes it.

The second one *feels* powerful because the document appears in a government database and can show up on a credit report or a lien search. But that appearance is the *only* thing it does on its own. It is not an adjudication of anything. Section 2 explains why.

---

### 2. What a "UCC Filing" Actually Is (and Isn't)

#### The mechanics

A **UCC-1 financing statement** is filed with the **Missouri Secretary of State**. Under **RSMo 400.9-501(a)(2)**, the filing office is "[t]he office of the secretary of state in all other cases" — i.e., for most personal-property collateral. (The exception in § 400.9-501(a)(1): if the collateral is as-extracted collateral, timber to be cut, or fixtures filed as a fixture filing, it goes to the county office that records real-property mortgages.)

Its whole job is **public notice**. It announces to anyone who searches the index: *this debtor has already granted this creditor a security interest in this collateral, so if you're thinking of lending against it, know that someone got there first.*

#### The critical point: a UCC-1 records a right that already exists — it does not create one

This is the heart of the matter, and it's where the "redemption"/"sovereign citizen" theories go wrong.

**Step one — the security interest has to exist first.** Under **RSMo 400.9-203(b)**, a security interest is enforceable against the debtor only when **all three** of these are true:

1. **"Value has been given"** — the creditor actually gave something (a loan, goods, credit).
2. **"[T]he debtor has rights in the collateral or the power to transfer rights in the collateral to a secured party"** — the debtor actually owns or can transfer the thing.
3. **The debtor "has authenticated a security agreement that provides a description of the collateral"** (§ 400.9-203(b)(3)(A)) — the debtor **signed or otherwise authenticated an agreement** granting the interest. (Missouri's text says *"authenticated"* rather than *"signed"* — that's broader than a pen signature and covers electronic adoption of a record, but it still requires an affirmative act **by the debtor**.)

**Step two — you may file only if the debtor authorized it.** **RSMo 400.9-509(a)** is explicit:

> "A person may file an initial financing statement, amendment that adds collateral covered by a financing statement, or amendment that adds a debtor to a financing statement **only if** ... [t]he debtor authorizes the filing in an authenticated record..."

And § 400.9-509(b) explains where that authorization normally comes from: **"By authenticating or becoming bound as debtor by a security agreement, a debtor ... authorizes the filing of an initial financing statement."**

So the security agreement is the engine; the UCC-1 is just the public receipt. **No security agreement → no authorization → no lawful filing.**

#### What a UCC-1 does *not* do

Filing a UCC-1, standing alone:

- **Does not create a debt.** It presupposes one. If nobody owes you anything, filing a piece of paper doesn't make them owe you.
- **Does not create a lien on a person or on property they didn't pledge.** A security interest comes from the *debtor's grant*, not from the creditor's filing.
- **Does not create a judgment.** Only a court does that, after a lawsuit with notice and an opportunity to be heard.
- **Is not "charging" anybody with anything.**
- **Is not reviewed for truth when filed.** The filing office indexes records; it does not adjudicate them. (It *can* refuse some — see § 400.9-516(b)(8)–(9) in Section 3.)

The Eighth Circuit put the practical reality bluntly in a case about exactly this kind of filing: a bogus UCC-1 would **"not have succeeded in perfecting a priority claim to any property as a matter of commercial law. But that is not a defense."** *United States v. Reed*, 668 F.3d 978 (8th Cir. 2012). In other words: **the filing is legally worthless *and* it can still send you to prison.** Worst of both worlds.

#### The flip side — if someone files a bogus UCC-1 against *you*

You have two clean tools, and they're worth knowing:

**(a) File an information statement — RSMo 400.9-518.** Any person may file an information statement about a record indexed under their name **"if the person believes that the record is inaccurate or was wrongfully filed."** It must identify the record by file number, say that it's an information statement, and explain the basis for the belief, "including the manner in which the person believes the record should be amended."

**Important limitation:** § 400.9-518 states that **"the filing of an information statement does not affect the effectiveness of an initial financing statement or other filed record."** It puts your side of the story in the file. It does not remove the bogus filing.

**(b) Ask the Secretary of State to terminate it — RSMo 400.9-516(e).** This is the stronger tool, and it's easy to miss. Added by 2013 H.B. 212 and amended in 2014, it provides:

> "In the alternative to the provisions of sections 428.105 through 428.135, if an information statement filed with the secretary of state under section 400.9-518 alleges that a previously filed record was wrongfully filed, the secretary of state shall, without undue delay, determine whether the contested record was wrongfully filed. ... If the secretary of state finds that the record was wrongfully filed, **the secretary of state shall terminate the record and the record shall be void and ineffective.**"

So: file the § 400.9-518 information statement alleging wrongful filing, and the Secretary of State must investigate and, if it was wrongful, **terminate it** — no lawsuit required. The office may ask you or the filer for more information, "including an original or copy of a security agreement that is related to the record." That's the whole ballgame: if the filer can't produce a security agreement you authenticated, the filing is wrongful.

---

### 3. The Serious Warning: When a UCC Filing Becomes a Crime

This section exists because the phrase "filing UCC charges against someone" is the exact vocabulary of a well-documented scheme that has put a lot of people in prison. I'm not assuming that's what you have in mind — but you asked whether there's "something special about the UCC," and this is the thing you most need to know before going near a UCC-1.

#### The scheme, briefly

There's a set of theories circulating — variously called "**redemption**," "**strawman**," "**Accepted for Value / A4V**," or generally "**sovereign citizen**" theory — which claim that filing a UCC-1 lets you assert a multimillion-dollar security interest against a judge, prosecutor, police officer, court clerk, ex-spouse, bank employee, or anyone else who has crossed you. The claimed mechanism is that the person "consented" through some silence, contract, or oath, so you can name them as your "debtor."

**None of it works.** Every court to consider it has rejected it. And prosecutors treat it as a crime — because that's what it is.

#### (a) Missouri law

##### RSMo 570.095 — Filing false documents (a FELONY, and the statute directly on point)

Enacted by **2018 H.B. 1769**, effective **August 28, 2018**, this is the statute that squarely covers bogus UCC filings. It's worth reading closely, because it names UCC filings explicitly.

**The offense — § 570.095.1:** A person commits the offense of filing false documents if:

> "(1) With the intent to defraud, deceive, harass, alarm, or negatively impact financially, or in such a manner reasonably calculated to deceive, defraud, harass, alarm, or negatively impact financially, he or she files, causes to be filed or recorded, or attempts to file or record, creates, uses as genuine, transfers or has transferred, presents, or prepares with knowledge or belief that it will be filed, presented, recorded, or transferred to the secretary of state or the secretary's designee, to the recorder of deeds ... to any municipal, county, district, or state government entity, division, agency, or office, or to any credit bureau or financial institution any of the following types of documents:
> (a) Common law lien;
> **(b) Uniform commercial code filing or record;**
> (c) Real property recording;
> **(d) Financing statement;**
> (e) Contract;
> (f) Warranty, special, or quitclaim deed; ...
> (i) Legal affidavit;
> (j) Legal process;
> (k) Legal summons; ...
> (m) Criminal charging documents or materially false criminal charging documents; ..."

**and** — § 570.095.1(2) — the document:

> "contains materially false information; is fraudulent; is a forgery, as defined under section 570.090; **lacks the consent of all parties listed in a document that requires mutual consent**; or is invalid under Missouri law."

Read subdivision (1)(b) and (1)(d) again: **"Uniform commercial code filing or record"** and **"Financing statement"** are named in the statute. And subdivision (2)'s "lacks the consent of all parties listed in a document that requires mutual consent" describes a UCC-1 filed against someone who never signed a security agreement almost word for word.

**The penalty — § 570.095.2:**

> "Filing false documents under this section is a **class D felony** for the first offense except the following circumstances shall be a **class C felony**: ..."

The class C felony enhancements apply when the victim or named party is (among others):

- an official **elected** to municipal, county, district, federal, or statewide office (§ 570.095.2(2)(a));
- an official **appointed** to such office, **or an employee of** such an official (§ 570.095.2(2)(b)–(c));
- **"a judge or magistrate of ... [a]ny court or division of the court in this or any other state or an employee thereof"** or of any U.S. court system (§ 570.095.2(3));
- **a full-time, part-time, reserve, or auxiliary peace officer** licensed in Missouri or any other state (§ 570.095.2(4));
- a **firefighter**, including volunteers (§ 570.095.2(5));
- federal **job class 1811** agents and federal law enforcement officers under 5 U.S.C. § 8401(17)(A) or (D) (§ 570.095.2(6)–(7));
- **"an employee of any law enforcement or legal prosecution agency"** in Missouri, another state, or the U.S. (§ 570.095.2(8));
- railroad police officers (§ 570.095.2(10));
- or the defendant has a **prior conviction** under this section (§ 570.095.2(1)).

**And it reaches retirees and family members.** § 570.095.3 provides that "a person who has retired or resigned from any agency, institution, or occupation listed under subsection 2 ... shall be considered the same as a person who remains in employment," and extends the enhancement to that person's **spouse**, their or their spouse's **ancestor or descendant by blood or adoption**, and their **stepchild** while the marriage exists. (§ 570.095.3 also requires that the victim's occupation be *material* to the document and materially connected to why they were named — so the enhancement isn't automatic.)

**Sentencing exposure (RSMo 558.011, version effective 8/28/2021, in force now):**

- **Class D felony:** "a term of years not to exceed **seven years**"
- **Class C felony:** "a term of years **not less than three years and not to exceed ten years**"
- Fines: up to **$10,000** for a class C, D, or E felony (RSMo 558.002.1(1))

**Mandatory restitution — § 570.095.4:** anyone found guilty "shall be ordered by the court to make full restitution to any person or entity that has sustained actual losses or costs.... Such restitution **shall not be paid in lieu of jail or prison time but rather in addition to** any jail or prison time imposed."

**Two provisions that close off the usual escape hatches:**

- **§ 570.095.5(2):** "**A filing or record being rejected by the receiving entity shall not be used as an affirmative defense.**" Getting rejected doesn't save you — the *attempt* is enough, and § 570.095.1(1) covers one who "attempts to file or record."
- **§ 570.095.5(1):** "Nothing in this section shall limit the power of the state to investigate, charge, or punish any person for any conduct that constitutes a crime by any other statute of this state or the United States." You can be charged under this *and* other statutes.

**And these filings are actively watched.** § 570.095.6(1) required every state agency, county, and city not within a county that receives filings — "including county recorders of deeds and **the secretary of state's office**" — to have implemented, by January 1, 2019, a system logging documents that "appear to be fraudulent or contain suspicious language." Those logs are available to prosecutors, sheriffs, police chiefs, and commissioned peace officers, and § 570.095.6(1) states that review "is permissible for the agent or agencies under this subdivision **without the need of a grand jury subpoena or court order**." Under § 570.095.6(2), the receiving entity must notify the county's chief law enforcement officer and prosecutor **within two business days**.

In plain terms: **the Secretary of State's office logs suspicious UCC filings and forwards them to prosecutors within two business days, and prosecutors can read the log without a subpoena.**

There is also a **civil** route in § 570.095.7–.9: a person can file a probable cause statement in the associate or circuit court of the county where the filing was received; the court sets an initial hearing **within twenty business days**; a ruling of invalid "shall be evidence that the original filing or record was not accurate, true, or correct," recording fees are waived, and under § 570.095.9, **"[i]f a filing or record is deemed invalid, court costs and fees are the responsibility of the party who originally initiated the filing or record."**

##### RSMo 575.133 — Filing a nonconsensual common law lien

A separate crime, enacted by 2014 S.B. 491 and **amended by 2025 H.B. 495, effective August 28, 2025**. A person commits it by filing "a document that purports to assert a lien against the assets, real or personal, of any person and that, regardless of any self-description":

> "(1) Is not expressly provided for by a specific state or federal statute;
> (2) Does not depend upon the consent of the owner of the property affected or the existence of a contract for its existence; and
> (3) Is not an equitable or constructive lien imposed by a state or federal court of competent jurisdiction."

**Penalty (§ 575.133.3), as amended in 2025:** a **class B misdemeanor**; **class A misdemeanor** for a second offense; **class E felony** for a third or subsequent offense, and a person convicted of a third or subsequent offense "shall be considered a persistent offender, as such term is defined in section 558.016."

*One nuance worth understanding:* element (1) requires that the lien **not** be "expressly provided for by a specific state or federal statute." A UCC-1 financing statement *is* expressly provided for by statute (Chapter 400), so there's a real argument that a bogus UCC-1 falls outside § 575.133 and is instead prosecuted under § 570.095 — which, as shown above, names UCC filings directly. Don't take comfort from that gap: **§ 570.095 covers it, and it's the felony.**

##### RSMo 575.130 — Simulating legal process

> "A person commits the offense of simulating legal process if, with purpose to mislead the recipient and cause him or her to take action in reliance thereon, he or she delivers or causes to be delivered: (1) A request for the payment of money on behalf of any creditor that in form and substance simulates any legal process issued by any court of this state; or (2) Any purported summons, subpoena or other legal process knowing that the process was not issued or authorized by any court."

**Penalty:** "The offense of simulating legal process is a **class B misdemeanor**" (§ 575.130.3) — up to **six months** (RSMo 558.011) and a fine up to **$1,000** (RSMo 558.002.1(3)). Exception: it doesn't apply to a subpoena properly issued by a notary public (§ 575.130.2).

This one catches the homemade "notices," "affidavits of obligation," and fake summonses that often accompany bogus UCC filings.

##### RSMo 428.105–428.135 — the civil side: getting a bogus lien killed and collecting for it

These sections (all from 1996 S.B. 869) deal with **"nonconsensual common law liens."** § 428.105 defines one as a document asserting a lien on assets that:

> "(a) Is not expressly provided for by a specific state or federal statute; (b) Does not depend upon the consent of the owner of the property affected or the existence of a contract for its existence; and (c) Is not an equitable or constructive lien imposed by a state or federal court."

"Filing officer" is defined to include the **secretary of state**, county recorders, and circuit clerks.

- **§ 428.110.1:** "Any filing officer **may reject** for filing or recording any nonconsensual common law lien" (with carve-outs for documents shown to be authorized by contract, lease, or statute, imposed by a court, or filed by a licensed attorney or a financial institution).
- **§ 428.110.2:** If one has already been accepted, the filing officer "**shall accept** for filing a sworn notice of invalid lien," captioned "Notice of Invalid Lien," filed by the person against whom the lien was filed. The filing officer must mail a copy to the lien claimant within one business day.
- **§ 428.120:** The property owner may petition the circuit court of the county where the lien was recorded for an order — **"which may be granted ex parte"** — directing the lien claimant to appear **within ten business days** of service and show cause why the lien should not be declared void. The petition must state the grounds and be supported by an affidavit.
- **§ 428.125.1–.2:** If the claimant fails to appear, "the claim of lien shall be declared **void ab initio** and released" and the claimant pays costs and reasonable attorney's fees. If the court finds after a hearing that it is a nonconsensual common law lien, "the court **shall** issue an order declaring the lien void ab initio, releasing the lien and awarding **costs and reasonable attorney's fees to the prevailing party**."
- **§ 428.135 — the money:** Any person who records or files a document "purporting to create a nonconsensual common law lien against real or personal property" **"shall be liable to the damaged party for actual damages or five thousand dollars, whichever is greater plus costs and reasonable attorney's fees."**

Missouri courts apply this. In *Stiens v. Stiens*, 231 S.W.3d 195 (Mo. App. W.D. 2007), a document styled "Notice of Claim and Proposed Lien" was declared **void ab initio**, and the property owners were awarded court costs, attorney's fees, and statutory damages.

The definition is genuinely narrow, though — it excludes anything consent-based. In *Myers v. Sander*, No. 4:13CV2192 CDP (E.D. Mo. Feb. 3, 2014), the court rejected an argument that a bank's deed of trust was a nonconsensual common law lien: **"Because Middlewest was the owner of 'the property affected' at the time it consented to and executed the First Bank deed of trust, that deed of trust cannot constitute a nonconsensual common law lien."**

##### RSMo 400.9-516 — the Secretary of State can refuse the filing outright

Missouri amended § 400.9-516 (2013 H.B. 212; 2014 H.B. 1376 merged with H.B. 1412, effective 8/28/2014) to let the filing office **refuse** records, and two of the grounds are aimed squarely at this problem. Under § 400.9-516(b), filing does not occur where the office refuses to accept the record because:

> "**(8) The secretary of state has reasonable cause to believe the record is materially false or fraudulent; or**
> **(9)** The record on its face reveals, based on factors such as whether the debtor and the secured party are substantially the same person, the individual debtor is a transmitting utility, or whether the collateral described is within the scope of this chapter, that the record is being filed for a purpose other than a transaction that is within the scope of this chapter. **This includes a record that asserts a claim against a current or former employee or officer of a federal, state, county, or other local governmental unit that relates to the performance of the officer's or employee's public duties, and for which the filer does not hold a properly executed security agreement or judgment from a court of competent jurisdiction.**"

Subdivision (9) is a description of the sovereign-citizen filing, written into the statute. Note also the "debtor and the secured party are substantially the same person" factor — that's aimed at "strawman" filings where a person names themselves in both roles.

And § 400.9-516(c)(3) adds a presumption for prison filings: a record "shall be **presumed** to be materially false or fraudulent if the document, instrument, or record is filed by an offender or on behalf of an offender," rebuttable only by producing "a sworn and notarized document signed by the obligor, debtor, or owner of the property designated as collateral stating that the person entered into a security agreement with the offender and authorized the filing." ("Offender" here means inmates in the custody of the Department of Corrections, per § 217.010.)

##### RSMo 400.9-625 — the UCC's own civil penalty for filing without authorization

Even inside the UCC itself, § 400.9-625(e) provides that "the debtor, consumer obligor, or person named as a debtor in a filed record, as applicable, may recover **five hundred dollars in each case**" from a person that, among other things:

> "(3) **Files a record that the person is not entitled to file under section 400.9-509(a)**"

That's on top of actual damages under § 400.9-625(b).

#### (b) Federal law: 18 U.S.C. § 1521 — up to 10 years

Congress made this a federal felony in 2008 (Pub. L. 110-177, title II, § 201(a), Jan. 7, 2008, 121 Stat. 2535). The section is titled **"Retaliating against a Federal judge or Federal law enforcement officer by false claim or slander of title"**:

> "Whoever files, attempts to file, or conspires to file, in any public record or in any private record which is generally available to the public, any false lien or encumbrance against the real or personal property of an individual described in section 1114, on account of the performance of official duties by that individual, knowing or having reason to know that such lien or encumbrance is false or contains any materially false, fictitious, or fraudulent statement or representation, shall be fined under this title or **imprisoned for not more than 10 years**, or both."

Points worth noting: it covers **attempts** and **conspiracies**, not just completed filings; it applies to *any* public record or publicly available private record; and the mental state is "**knowing or having reason to know**" — willful blindness won't help.

#### (c) Courts and prosecutors treat these filings as crimes, not as legal strategy

***United States v. Reed*, 668 F.3d 978 (8th Cir. 2012)** — the controlling circuit for Missouri. Gregory Allen Davis filed a **Form UCC-1 financing statement** with the D.C. Recorder of Deeds naming **U.S. District Judge Daniel Hovland** and **Acting U.S. Attorney Lynn Jordheim** as debtors, claiming "$2,400,100.00 USD for default of court case" plus "$1,000,000.00 (million) in sliver [sic] coinage." Davis and Michael Howard Reed were convicted under **18 U.S.C. § 1521**; Reed was also convicted of obstruction under 18 U.S.C. § 1503(a). The Eighth Circuit **affirmed**. The court's key observation, quoted above, deserves repeating: the filing would "not have succeeded in perfecting a priority claim to any property as a matter of commercial law. **But that is not a defense.**"

**Missouri has prosecuted this.** In the **Western District of Missouri**, a Kansas City couple, Roderick and Amber Moore, were sentenced by Judge Dean Whipple in a "sovereign citizen" scheme involving fraudulent financial instruments and false liens against federal entities; Amber Moore "filed false liens in the state of Washington against the U.S. Bankruptcy Court." Roderick Moore received 13 months in federal prison without parole; Amber Moore received three years' probation. Their plea agreements prohibited them from "filing any false liens, false 1099's, false tax returns **or other Uniform Commercial Code documents related to sovereign citizens**."

#### The bottom line for Section 3

**Filing a UCC-1 against a judge, prosecutor, police officer, court clerk, ex-partner, landlord, bank employee, opposing party, or anyone else who did not sign a security agreement with you is not a legal remedy.** It accomplishes nothing legally — the Eighth Circuit said as much — and it exposes you to:

- a **class D or class C felony** under RSMo 570.095 (up to 7 or 10 years, $10,000 fine, mandatory restitution);
- a misdemeanor-to-felony ladder under RSMo 575.133;
- a class B misdemeanor under RSMo 575.130 for accompanying fake process;
- **civil liability of the greater of actual damages or $5,000 plus attorney's fees** under RSMo 428.135;
- **$500 plus actual damages** under RSMo 400.9-625(b) and (e)(3);
- and, if a federal judge or federal law enforcement officer is the target, **up to 10 years in federal prison** under 18 U.S.C. § 1521.

It also does something quieter but very damaging to someone in your position: it destroys your credibility with the judge in your *real* case. A litigant with a legitimate conversion claim who files a bogus lien stops being read as a person with a grievance and starts being read as a person to be managed. If you have a genuine claim over the Jeep — and the next section suggests you may — that is the thing worth protecting.

---

### 4. How the UCC Legitimately Can Help You — Including the Jeep

Now the constructive part. The UCC is genuinely useful to you, and some of it bears directly on a Jeep sale and a conversion claim.

#### Article 2 (Sales) applies — a used Jeep is "goods"

**RSMo 400.2-105(1)** defines goods as "all things (including specially manufactured goods) which are **movable at the time of identification to the contract for sale** other than the money in which the price is to be paid, investment securities (article 8) and things in action."

A vehicle is movable, so it's goods, and Article 2 governs the sale — **including a used one**. Missouri courts have confirmed this: an annotation to § 400.2-316 notes that the "[s]ale of used or second-hand goods is covered by provisions of the Uniform Commercial Code and an implied warranty of merchantability may arise." *Worthey v. Specialty Foam Products, Inc.*, 591 S.W.2d 145 (Mo. App. 1979).

#### The deadline — RSMo 400.2-725 (four years) — read this carefully

**§ 400.2-725(1):** "An action for breach of any contract for sale must be commenced **within four years** after the cause of action has accrued. By the original agreement the parties may reduce the period of limitation to not less than one year but may not extend it."

**§ 400.2-725(2)** is the part that catches people: "A cause of action accrues **when the breach occurs, regardless of the aggrieved party's lack of knowledge of the breach**. A breach of warranty occurs **when tender of delivery is made**," except where a warranty explicitly extends to future performance and discovery must await that performance.

So the four-year clock generally starts at **delivery** — not when you discovered the problem. **Work out your delivery date and count forward four years.** Note also that this is the limitations period for a *contract/sale* claim. A **conversion** claim is a tort with its own limitations period under a different statute (RSMo Chapter 516), which I did not research here — **confirm the conversion deadline separately**, because the two are not the same and the shorter one controls whichever claim it applies to.

**§ 400.2-725(3)** offers a small safety net: if a timely action is terminated in a way that leaves another remedy available, a new action may be brought within **six months** after termination — unless it was voluntarily dismissed or dismissed for failure to prosecute.

#### Buyer's and seller's remedies

- **Buyer:** § 400.2-711 ("Buyer's remedies in general — buyer's security interest in rejected goods") lets a buyer, where the seller fails to deliver, repudiates, or where the buyer rightfully rejects or justifiably revokes acceptance, **cancel** and recover "so much of the price as has been paid," and in addition "**cover**" and get damages under § 400.2-712. The buyer's remedy sections run **§§ 400.2-711 to 400.2-717**.
- **Seller:** the corresponding seller's remedy sections run **§§ 400.2-703 to 400.2-710**.

#### Warranties and disclaimers — RSMo 400.2-316

§ 400.2-316 governs exclusion or modification of warranties (the "as is" language). Two Missouri-specific points I verified:

- **§ 400.2-316(4):** remedies for breach of warranty can be limited under §§ 400.2-718 and 400.2-719.
- **§ 400.2-316(5) — Missouri's livestock variant** (added by 1980 H.B. 972): "A seller is not liable for damages resulting from the lack of merchantability or fitness for a particular purpose of **livestock** he sells if the contract for the sale of the livestock does not contain a written statement as to a warranty of merchantability or fitness." This is a Missouri deviation from the uniform text — it doesn't affect a Jeep, but it's a good illustration that **you must read Missouri's version, not a generic UCC summary**, because states change the text.

#### Title and the good-faith purchaser — RSMo 400.2-401 and 400.2-403

**§ 400.2-401** ("Passing of title — reservation for security — limited application of this section") sets out when title passes, but opens with an important caveat: the article's rules on "rights, obligations and remedies of the seller, the buyer, purchasers or other third parties applies **irrespective of title** to the goods except where the provision refers to such title." In other words, under Article 2 you often don't need to win the title argument to win the remedy argument.

**§ 400.2-403** is the one that matters when a vehicle gets **resold to someone else**:

> "(1) A purchaser of goods acquires all title which his transferor had or had power to transfer.... **A person with voidable title has power to transfer a good title to a good faith purchaser for value.** When goods have been delivered under a transaction of purchase the purchaser has such power even though (a) the transferor was deceived as to the identity of the purchaser, or (b) the delivery was in exchange for a check which is later dishonored, or (c) it was agreed that the transaction was to be a 'cash sale', or **(d) the delivery was procured through fraud punishable as larcenous under the criminal law.**"

Subsection (d) is worth sitting with. If you handed over the Jeep and the person who took it defrauded you, that person may hold **voidable** title — and if they resold it to an innocent buyer who paid value in good faith, **that buyer can end up with good title**, and your remedy runs against the fraudster, not the innocent buyer. That's a hard rule but it's the law, and it shapes who you should be suing.

**§ 400.2-403(2)–(3) — entrustment:** "Any entrusting of possession of goods to a **merchant who deals in goods of that kind** gives him power to transfer all rights of the entruster to a **buyer in ordinary course of business**." And "entrusting" includes "any delivery and any acquiescence in retention of possession regardless of any condition expressed between the parties ... and regardless of whether the procurement of the entrusting or the possessor's disposition of the goods have been such as to be larcenous under the criminal law." **If you left the Jeep with a dealer and the dealer sold it, this section may cut against you.**

#### Article 9, if you ever finance or secure a vehicle

- **§ 400.9-609** — after default, a secured party "[m]ay take possession of the collateral," and may do so "(1) Pursuant to judicial process; or (2) **Without judicial process, if it proceeds without breach of the peace.**" That "breach of the peace" limit is the whole ballgame in repossession disputes — a repo agent who breaks into a locked garage, or proceeds over your contemporaneous objection, may have breached it.
- **§§ 400.9-610 to 400.9-625** govern disposition of collateral after repossession and the debtor's rights, with the remedies in § 400.9-625 discussed above (actual damages under (b); $500 statutory damages under (e)).

#### Article 3, for checks and notes

- **§ 400.3-302** defines "**holder in due course**" — a holder who took the instrument for value, in good faith, and without notice of dishonor or defenses, where the instrument didn't bear apparent evidence of forgery or alteration. A holder in due course takes free of most defenses, which is why it matters who ends up holding a note.
- **§ 400.3-502** covers dishonor.

#### ⚠️ The thing that may matter more than the UCC: RSMo 301.210

**This is the most important practical point in this whole document for your Jeep claim.** Missouri's certificate-of-title statute can override the UCC analysis entirely, and it has sunk conversion claims before.

**RSMo 301.210.1** requires that on a sale or transfer, "the holder of such certificate shall endorse on the same an assignment thereof, with warranty of title in form printed thereon ... and **deliver the same to the buyer at the time of the delivery** to him of such motor vehicle."

**RSMo 301.210.4** — the current text, effective **August 28, 2020** (2020 H.B. 1963):

> "It shall be unlawful for any person to buy or sell in this state any motor vehicle or trailer registered under the laws of this state, unless, at the time of the delivery thereof, there shall pass between the parties such certificates of ownership with an assignment thereof, as provided in this section, and the sale of any motor vehicle or trailer registered under the laws of this state, without the assignment of such certificate of ownership, shall be **presumed fraudulent and void** unless the parties have executed a written agreement for delayed delivery of certificate of ownership as provided in subsection 5 of this section."

**Why this can be fatal to a conversion claim.** Missouri courts have long held that a buyer who never got an assigned title **never became the owner** — and conversion requires ownership or the right to possession. In *Minton v. Hill*, 944 S.W.2d 250 (Mo. App. W.D. 1997), the Western District held that **"a buyer of a used automobile who has not received a certificate of title acquires neither ownership nor the right to possession of the motor vehicle,"** and **rejected the conversion claim** on exactly that basis, because conversion requires "the right to possession of the property that was converted." The court instead remanded for **restitution of the $2,500 purchase price**.

The older annotations under § 301.210 point the same way — e.g., *Galati v. New Amsterdam Casualty Co.*, 381 S.W.2d 5 (Mo. App. 1964) ("Where no certificate of title was delivered to purported purchaser of automobile at the time the purchase money was paid, the sale was void"); *Moore v. State Farm Mutual Automobile Insurance Co.*, 381 S.W.2d 161 (Mo. App. 1964) (such a purchaser "not only does not become the owner thereof but also does not acquire any insurable interest therein"); and *Matthews v. Truxton Parts, Inc.*, 327 S.W.2d 28 (Mo. App. 1959) (so long as title is not assigned and delivered, "the buyer may repudiate his contract of purchase and may recover what he has paid if within a reasonable time he returns the vehicle"). *Reddish v. Heartland Auto Plaza*, No. 27392 (Mo. App. S.D. Aug. 4, 2006), holds that a valid sale requires **two contemporaneous acts** — delivery of the vehicle *and* delivery of the properly assigned certificate.

**But — and this is a real opening — the statute changed in 2020.** The pre-2020 text (which every one of those cases was applying) said the sale "shall be **fraudulent and void**," flat. The 2020 amendment changed it to "shall be **presumed** fraudulent and void," and added the delayed-delivery-agreement exception. A **presumption** is rebuttable; an absolute voidness is not. **I found no Missouri appellate decision construing the 2020 "presumed" language.** That means:

1. If the older, absolute rule still effectively governs, and you were the **buyer** without an assigned title, a straight conversion claim is in serious trouble — and you should be framing the claim as **rescission/restitution of the purchase price** (as *Minton* and *Matthews* did), plus contract and warranty claims under Article 2, plus possibly a Merchandising Practices Act claim (below).
2. If the 2020 amendment softened the rule to a rebuttable presumption, there may be room to argue you did acquire an interest despite the title defect. **This is an open question and a genuinely arguable point** — but you'd be arguing it on fresh ground, so brief it properly rather than assuming it.
3. If you were the **seller** and never assigned title, the analysis flips, and § 301.210's effect on who still owns the Jeep may actually *help* you.

**Also check § 301.210.5:** a licensed dealer may deliver a vehicle with a **written agreement** to pass the assigned title within **thirty days** after delivery. If the dealer misses that window, "the sale shall be **voidable at purchaser's option**" and the dealer must repurchase the vehicle.

**Practical takeaway:** before you invest more in a conversion theory, pin down (a) exactly what happened with the certificate of title and when, (b) whether any written delayed-delivery agreement exists, and (c) whether the other side was a licensed dealer. Those three facts may matter more to the outcome than anything in Chapter 400.

#### One more avenue: the Merchandising Practices Act

If the Jeep was purchased **primarily for personal, family, or household purposes**, **RSMo 407.025.1(1)** gives you a **private civil action** for actual damages against a seller who used a method, act, or practice declared unlawful by § 407.020, filed "in either the circuit court of the county in which the seller or lessor resides or in which the transaction complained of took place."

Note the 2020 amendments added proof requirements at **§ 407.025.1(2)**: you must establish "(a) That the person acted as a reasonable consumer would in light of all circumstances; (b) That the method, act, or practice declared unlawful by section 407.020 would cause a reasonable person to enter into the transaction that resulted in damages; and (c) **Individual damages with sufficiently definitive and objective evidence to allow the loss to be calculated with a reasonable degree of certainty.**" That third one is a real burden — document your damages carefully.

The MMPA is often a better fit than the UCC for a deceptive used-vehicle sale, and it's worth pricing out alongside your conversion and contract theories.

---

### 5. Civil vs. Criminal, Side by Side

|  | **CIVIL** | **CRIMINAL** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Who brings it** | A **private party** (you) files a *petition* stating *claims* against another private party. | **The government only** — a county Prosecuting Attorney, the St. Louis Circuit Attorney, the Attorney General, or a U.S. Attorney — files *charges*. A private person **cannot** file criminal charges. You can report a crime; the decision to charge belongs to the prosecutor. |
| **What it's called** | Petition, complaint, claim, cause of action, count | Charge, indictment, information, complaint |
| **Standard of proof** | **Preponderance of the evidence** — more likely than not (just over 50%). Some issues (e.g., fraud) require **clear and convincing evidence**. | **Beyond a reasonable doubt** — the highest standard in law. |
| **What you can win / lose** | **Money damages**, return of property (**replevin**), **rescission/restitution**, **injunctions**, declaratory judgments, foreclosure on collateral, and sometimes costs and attorney's fees. Nobody goes to jail. | **Fine, probation, or imprisonment**, plus restitution, plus a criminal record. The victim doesn't "win" anything directly — restitution aside, the case belongs to the State. |
| **Who controls it** | You do. You can settle, dismiss, or amend. | The prosecutor does. A victim cannot "drop the charges." |
| **Right to a lawyer** | No right to appointed counsel — you hire one or go pro se. | Right to appointed counsel if you can't afford one. |
| **Where the UCC sits** | **Here — entirely.** RSMo Chapter 400 creates **private rights and civil remedies only**: damages (§ 400.2-711, § 400.9-625(b)), statutory damages (§ 400.9-625(e)), court orders (§ 400.9-625(a)), repossession and foreclosure of collateral (§§ 400.9-609, 400.9-610). | **Nowhere.** Chapter 400 defines **no crimes** and imposes **no criminal penalties**. |
| **Where commercial *crimes* actually live** | — | **RSMo Chapter 570** (Robbery, Arson, Burglary and Related Offenses) and **Chapter 575** (Offenses Against the Administration of Justice), in Title XXXVIII. See below. |

#### Where the crimes involving commercial paper actually are

| Statute | Offense | Note |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **RSMo 570.095** | **Filing false documents** — expressly includes "Uniform commercial code filing or record" and "Financing statement" | **Class D felony**; **class C felony** if the target is a judge, elected/appointed official, peace officer, prosecutor's-office employee, etc. Mandatory restitution. |
| **RSMo 575.133** | Filing a nonconsensual common law lien | Class B misdemeanor → class A misdemeanor (2d) → class E felony (3d+), as amended eff. 8/28/2025 |
| **RSMo 575.130** | Simulating legal process | Class B misdemeanor |
| **RSMo 570.090** | **Forgery** — "with the purpose to defraud," making, completing, altering, or authenticating a writing "so that it purports to have been made by another ... or by authority of one who did not give such authority," or using such a writing as genuine |  |
| **RSMo 570.120** | **Passing bad checks** — with purpose to defraud, making, issuing, or passing a check "knowing that it will not be paid by the drawee, or that there is no such drawee" | Includes provisions on notice, administrative handling costs, and service charges |
| **RSMo 570.130** | **Fraudulent use of a credit device or debit device** — using a device knowing it is "stolen, fictitious or forged," revoked or cancelled, or otherwise unauthorized |  |
| **RSMo 570.030** | Stealing | The general theft statute |
| **18 U.S.C. § 1521** | Federal false lien against a federal judge or federal law enforcement officer | **Up to 10 years** |

#### And the hybrid: RSMo Chapter 407 (Merchandising Practices Act)

Worth knowing because it doesn't fit neatly in either column:

- **Public enforcement:** the **Attorney General** enforces § 407.020 through **civil** actions — injunctions, restitution, civil penalties. That's the government suing, but it's a civil case, not a prosecution.
- **Private enforcement:** **§ 407.025** gives an individual consumer a **private civil action** for actual damages, plus (in the court's discretion) punitive damages and attorney's fees, and authorizes class actions.

So the MMPA is *civil* law that the government can also enforce. It is not criminal law, and an AG enforcement action is not a "charge."

---

### Practical Summary

1. **The UCC is civil.** It is Missouri's RSMo Chapter 400. It gives private parties private remedies enforced by lawsuits. It creates no crimes.
2. **"UCC charges" is not a thing.** Charges are criminal and only prosecutors file them. What you file is a **petition** stating **claims**.
3. **A UCC-1 filing is not a lawsuit, a lien on a person, a debt, or a judgment.** It's a public notice of a security interest the debtor already granted. Without an authenticated security agreement (§ 400.9-203(b)) and the debtor's authorization (§ 400.9-509(a)), filing one is unauthorized.
4. **Filing a UCC-1 against someone who didn't sign a security agreement is a felony in Missouri** — RSMo 570.095, class D, or class C if the target is a judge, official, or officer — plus civil liability of at least $5,000 plus attorney's fees (§ 428.135) and $500 plus damages (§ 400.9-625(e)(3)), plus up to 10 years federally if a federal judge or federal officer is the target (18 U.S.C. § 1521). It also does not work: *United States v. Reed*, 668 F.3d 978 (8th Cir. 2012).
5. **If someone files a bogus UCC-1 against *you***, use § 400.9-518 (information statement) plus § 400.9-516(e) — the Secretary of State must investigate and **terminate** a wrongfully filed record.
6. **For the Jeep, the UCC genuinely helps** — Article 2 governs the sale (§ 400.2-105), you have buyer's remedies (§§ 400.2-711 to 2-717), a **four-year** limitations period running from **delivery** (§ 400.2-725), and § 400.2-403 tells you who to sue when goods have been resold.
7. **But check RSMo 301.210 first.** A Missouri vehicle sale without assignment of the certificate of title is "presumed fraudulent and void," and under *Minton v. Hill*, 944 S.W.2d 250 (Mo. App. W.D. 1997), a buyer without title "acquires neither ownership nor the right to possession" — **which defeats a conversion claim**. Whether the 2020 amendment ("presumed") changes that is an open question. **Consider pleading rescission/restitution and MMPA claims in the alternative**, and get the title facts nailed down before you commit to a conversion theory.
8. **Watch your deadlines.** Four years from delivery for the sale/contract claim (§ 400.2-725), and a **separate** limitations period for conversion under RSMo Chapter 516 that I have not verified — confirm it now, not later.

---
